hy4-preview/09-incident-analysis/RESPONSE.md

Response — confidence and next data requests

Confidence level

Moderate-to-high (roughly 75–80%) that the eu-west backlog was caused by a zero-delay retry loop: the tenant_retry_policy rollout resolving to an empty policy (policy=""), with the retry path treating that empty value as delay_ms=0 instead of falling back to config_retry_default="exponential".

Confidence is not higher because:

  • The evidence set is 13 log lines and one timeline. There is no code, no configuration, and no full log export.
  • The central mechanism is a co-occurrence (policy="" alongside delay_ms=0), not a demonstrated code path.
  • Exactly one affected tenant (acme) and one clean tenant (beta) are named, so the affected population is unquantified.
  • One observation, the four minutes of normal processing after the 09:31 restart, is not fully explained by this account and may indicate an additional contributing mechanism.

What I hold with higher confidence:

  • It was not worker capacity. CPU fell 65% → 18% while the queue grew, and scaling 40 → 90 made the queue rise faster. This is strong evidence against the capacity hypothesis and was the basis of a harmful mitigation.
  • The feature flag, not the API deploy alone, governed the ongoing behaviour. The rollback at 09:24 fixed job creation but the backlog persisted for ~19 minutes, and a worker starting at 09:31:10 (after the rollback, with feature_flag=true) still reproduced the fault.
  • Database pool exhaustion was an amplifier, not the trigger. It appeared after the retry anomaly, no database errors appear in the logs, and the backlog cleared without any change to pool size.

What I hold with low confidence:

  • Whether an underlying dependency problem (H4) contributed anything at all. Nothing in the evidence supports it beyond the bare fact that retries occurred.
  • The active=10 idle=0 with wait_ms=5000 observation. This does not fit simple saturation at a 300 ceiling and is genuinely unexplained.

Two pieces of data I would request next

1. Complete worker and API logs for 08:55–10:30 UTC, including the resolved policy value for every tenant, per-attempt delay_ms, and the terminal state of every job that reached retry_budget_exhausted.

This is the highest-value request because it directly tests the root-cause hypothesis and closes the largest gaps at once. It would let me: confirm that empty policy maps to zero delay rather than merely co-occurring with it; enumerate which and how many tenants were affected; determine whether jobs were dropped, dead-lettered, duplicated, or externally retried (currently unknown, and the blocker on any customer-impact statement); and check whether the empty-policy condition also occurred in the US 2% cohort, which is the only way to turn the US/eu-west comparison from a suggestive anecdote into a real dose–response signal.

2. Per-pool database connection metrics for the same window — active, idle, wait time, and acquisition failures, broken down by client (worker pools vs. API) — together with the retry-policy configuration diff and flag-evaluation records for the 09:02 deploy.

The pool metrics would separate pool exhaustion from the retry loop, quantify how much of the outage was amplification versus primary cause, and explain the anomalous active=10 idle=0 reading. The config and flag-evaluation records would address the single most important remaining "why": whether the empty policy came from a misconfiguration, a missing default, or a propagation failure. That distinction determines whether the fix in B1 is sufficient or whether B4 (validation at rollout time) is the real control.

Note on method

I have deliberately kept observed facts and inference in separate sections, and have recorded the US region's lack of alerts as "no alert fired" rather than "no impact," since a 2% exposure with no alerting on the mechanism does not establish that the region was unaffected. I have not attributed the incident to any individual; the contributing factors are properties of the system's defaults, rollout controls, and alerting coverage.

hy4-preview/09-incident-analysis/ACTIONS.md

Actions — eu-west queue backlog

Owners are expressed as roles, not individuals. Every item states how success is verified and how it can be backed out. Actions are ordered by priority, not by team.

A. Immediate containment (do first / already in progress)

A1. Keep tenant_retry_policy disabled in eu-west

  • Owner: Release / Feature-flag owner, with On-call engineer.
  • Why: This is the only intervention observed to restore normal retry behaviour (delay_ms=0 → delay_ms=1000, job success within 2 minutes).
  • Verification: Per-tenant retry delay is ≥ the configured backoff floor; retry_budget_exhausted rate returns to pre-09:07 baseline; queue depth monotonically decreasing; worker CPU returning toward 65%.
  • Rollback: Re-enabling would recreate the fault. If it must be re-enabled for a specific tenant, re-enable for a single non-production or consenting tenant only, with the delay floor from B2 already shipped.

A2. Confirm the API rollback is complete and consistent across all api instances

  • Owner: Release engineer.
  • Why: Job creation normalized at 09:24, but the backlog persisted; confirm no instance was missed so the two control surfaces (deploy, flag) do not diverge.
  • Verification: All api hosts report 2026.09.23.1 absent; no new jobs enqueued with an empty policy value.
  • Rollback: Redeploy the previous release channel. Note that rolling forward to 2026.09.23.1 without B1/B2 will reintroduce the fault.

A3. Stop scaling workers as a response to this incident

  • Owner: On-call engineer.
  • Why: 40 → 90 made queue depth rise faster by adding connection contention.
  • Verification: Worker count at or below the pre-incident 40 while the backlog drains, then scaled back up only after A1 is confirmed effective and pool headroom is verified.
  • Rollback: Not applicable; this is a "stop doing the harmful thing" action.

A4. Drain the backlog without amplifying retries

  • Owner: On-call engineer + Queue service owner.
  • Why: Backlog jobs already carry the bad retry state; draining them at full concurrency risks re-triggering pool exhaustion.
  • Verification: Backlog depth decreasing with DB connections staying below the 300 ceiling and pool wait time under the alert threshold.
  • Rollback: Reduce drain concurrency to zero; the queue is durable, so pausing is safe.

A5. Preserve evidence

  • Owner: On-call engineer.
  • Why: Analysis rests on 13 log lines and one timeline. Log rotation may destroy the rest.
  • Verification: Full worker, API, and database pool logs for 08:55–10:30 UTC exported to durable storage before rotation.
  • Rollback: Not applicable.

B. Corrective — P0 (before the flag is ever re-enabled)

B1. Make an empty or unresolvable tenant policy fall back to the configured default

  • Owner: Queue service owner (with code reviewer).
  • Why: policy="" produced delay_ms=0 while config_retry_default="exponential" was available and unused.
  • Verification: Unit test asserting that policy="", unknown tenant, and missing-config all yield the default exponential schedule; integration test reproducing job=81 (three attempts, empty policy) now shows non-zero increasing delays.
  • Rollback: Feature-flagged code change; revert via standard deploy rollback. Note the flag cannot be used as the rollback here, since flag-on was the faulty path.

B2. Enforce a minimum retry delay (backoff floor)

  • Owner: Queue service owner.
  • Why: A retry budget consumed in 41 ms provides no protection. A floor makes the failure mode structurally impossible regardless of policy content.
  • Verification: No retry with delay_ms below the floor is observable in logs or metrics; alerting on any observed violation.
  • Rollback: Config-only change; revert the floor value. Keep the floor in place during any subsequent retry-policy rollout.

B3. Add alerting on retry-budget exhaustion and zero-delay retries

  • Owner: Observability owner.
  • Why: The fault was visible in logs at 09:06:58 but alerts only fired at 09:11 for downstream symptoms, ~4 minutes later, and nothing alerted on the mechanism.
  • Verification: Synthetic injection of an empty-policy job raises the alert within one minute and pages the queue service owner.
  • Rollback: Silence or delete the alert rule; no production behaviour change.

B4. Validate tenant policy resolution at rollout time

  • Owner: Release engineer + Config owner.
  • Why: Whether the empty policy came from misconfiguration, a missing default, or a propagation failure is still unknown (see unknowns 1 and 2).
  • Verification: A pre-rollout check enumerates every tenant and fails closed if any resolves to empty or unresolvable.
  • Rollback: Abort the rollout; the check is a gate, not a mutation.

C. Corrective — P1 (blast radius and amplification)

C1. Decouple worker concurrency from the shared database connection pool

  • Owner: Platform / Database owner, with Queue service owner.
  • Why: Scaling 40 → 90 workers drove connections to the 300 ceiling; pool pressure then blocked the API. One component's retry behaviour should not exhaust a shared resource.
  • Verification: Load test at 2× current worker count shows DB connections staying within a configured per-pool budget and API connection acquisition unaffected.
  • Rollback: Revert pool allocation config. This change should be staged (separate pool or a concurrency limiter) rather than a hard cutover.

C2. Investigate the active=10 idle=0 with 5-second wait observation

  • Owner: Database owner.
  • Why: Ten active connections alongside 5-second waits does not fit simple saturation at a 300 limit, and may indicate leaked, orphaned, or long-held connections.
  • Verification: Written finding explaining the observation, or a fix with a before/after comparison of pool wait time at fixed concurrency.
  • Rollback: Diagnostic action; any resulting fix follows normal rollback.

C3. Add per-tenant retry rate limiting

  • Owner: Queue service owner.
  • Why: The US region at 2% exposure did not alert while eu-west did, showing exposure fraction is the real blast-radius lever. A per-tenant cap contains even an unknown future policy defect to a small share of traffic.
  • Verification: Load test: a single tenant generating pathological retries cannot reduce another tenant's successful throughput.
  • Rollback: Config-only; raise or remove the cap.

C4. Make region and tenant exposure an explicit first-class rollout control

  • Owner: Release engineer + Feature-flag owner.
  • Why: The same release behaved differently in two regions purely because of flag exposure, which was discovered only after the fact.
  • Verification: Any retry-policy rollout records its exposure percentage per region in the change record and is staged 1% → 10% → 50% → 100% with a defined abort threshold at each step.
  • Rollback: Set exposure to 0.

D. Corrective — P2 (process and detection)

D1. Add a runbook entry: "queue growing while worker CPU falls"

  • Owner: On-call engineer + Queue service owner.
  • Why: The signature is inverted from normal capacity pressure and led to the harmful scale-up at 09:14.
  • Verification: Runbook merged and referenced from the job-age alert; a tabletop exercise shows the responder checks retry-delay metrics before scaling.
  • Rollback: Documentation only.

D2. Capture terminal job state for retry-exhausted jobs

  • Owner: Queue service owner + Observability owner.
  • Why: Whether jobs were dropped, dead-lettered, duplicated, or externally retried is currently unknown, which blocks any customer-impact statement.
  • Verification: Every job reaching retry-budget exhaustion emits a terminal state event; the disposition of job=81 and job=319 can be reconstructed retroactively or is confirmed unrecoverable.
  • Rollback: Logging/metric change; disable the emitter.

D3. Post-incident review

  • Owner: Incident commander.
  • Why: Multiple contributing factors (missing fallback, no backoff floor, coupled rollout controls, delayed detection) warrant a blameless review.
  • Verification: Review held, actions B1–C4 assigned with dates.
  • Rollback: Not applicable.

Cross-cutting notes

  • Do not re-enable tenant_retry_policy anywhere until B1, B2, B3, and B4 are complete and verified. The US region's 2% exposure is not evidence of safety — we do not know whether the empty-policy condition occurred there.
  • Ordering constraint: C1 (pool decoupling) should land before any future increase in worker concurrency, otherwise worker scaling remains an amplifier.
  • Sequencing risk: B1 and B2 both touch retry timing. Ship them as separate changes so that if recovery regresses, the cause is attributable.

hy4-preview/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Service update — delayed checkout in eu-west

Status: Resolved

Between 09:07 and 10:18 UTC on 23 September, some customers in eu-west experienced slower-than-normal checkout and order processing. Service is now normal and the backlog is cleared.

What happened: A change released at 09:02 UTC affected how our system retries work that does not finish first time. For some accounts, retries were made far too quickly instead of being spaced out. Those rapid retries consumed capacity without completing orders, so the queue grew.

What we did: We rolled the change back, then turned off the setting itself. Processing recovered within minutes, and all queued orders finished by 10:18 UTC.

Your orders: We believe orders placed during this window were delayed rather than cancelled, and we are confirming this. If you placed an order and did not receive confirmation, contact support and we will check it.

Next steps: We are fixing how unconfigured retry settings are handled, setting a minimum wait between retries, improving monitoring, and rolling out new settings more gradually.

We are sorry for the disruption.

hy4-preview/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident analysis — eu-west queue backlog

Incident ID: (unnamed) — eu-west job queue backlog and checkout latency Window (UTC): 2026-09-23 09:07 (first observable degradation) → 10:18 (backlog cleared) Sources: TIMELINE.md, LOGS.txt (13-line excerpt, not a complete log set)

1. Impact

Observed:

  • Queue depth in eu-west rose from 09:07 and did not return to baseline until 10:18 (~71 minutes).
  • Alerts fired at 09:11 for job age and checkout latency — i.e. the backlog was customer-visible, not only internal.
  • Worker CPU fell from 65% to 18% while the queue grew — workers were consuming less compute while more work was waiting, i.e. useful throughput collapsed.
  • Database connections reached the configured maximum of 300 by 09:20; at 09:19:52 the API logged db remaining connection slots reserved.
  • New job creation did not normalize until the API deploy was rolled back at 09:24; the existing backlog did not drain until the feature flag was disabled at 09:43 (~19 minutes later).

Not established from the available evidence:

  • The number of customers or orders affected, the peak queue depth, or how many checkouts failed outright versus were merely slow.
  • Whether any jobs were lost or duplicated rather than delayed. Logs show retry_budget_exhausted but no terminal job state, so the final disposition of jobs such as job=81 and job=319 is unknown.
  • Whether the 300-connection saturation affected other services sharing that database.
  • The US region is recorded as having no alert, but it had the flag enabled for only 2% of tenants; "no alert fired" is not the same as "no impact".

2. Timeline (UTC)

Time Event Type
09:02 Deploy of api 2026.09.23.1; change adds tenant-specific retry policy. Observed
09:06:58 worker-17: job=81 tenant=acme attempts 1–3 all result=retry delay_ms=0 within one second, then retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=41. Observed
09:07:01 worker-22: db pool wait exceeded wait_ms=5000 active=10 idle=0. Observed
09:07:03 worker-08: job=92 tenant=beta result=success elapsed_ms=830 — a different tenant processing normally at the same moment. Observed
09:07 Queue depth begins rising in eu-west; worker CPU falls 65% → 18%. Observed
09:11 Alerts fire for job age and checkout latency. Observed
09:14 On-call increases workers 40 → 90; queue depth rises faster. Observed
09:19:52 api-03: db remaining connection slots reserved. Observed
09:20 Database connections reach configured maximum of 300. Observed
09:24 API deploy rolled back; new job creation normalizes; backlog remains stuck. Observed
09:31 One worker pool restarted; processes normally for ~4 minutes, then stalls. Observed
09:31:10 worker-51 startup: config_retry_default="exponential" feature_flag=true. Observed
09:35:12 worker-51: job=319 retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=38. Observed
09:43 Feature flag tenant_retry_policy disabled. Observed
09:43:07 worker-51: config_changed feature_flag=false. Observed
09:43:09 job=319 attempt=1 result=retry delay_ms=1000. Observed
09:43:10 job=319 attempt=2 result=success elapsed_ms=190. Observed
09:45 Recovery begins (within ~2 minutes of flag disable). Observed
10:18 Backlog cleared. Observed

Context: the US region ran the same release but with the flag enabled for only 2% of tenants and no alert fired.

3. Most likely root cause

The tenant_retry_policy rollout resolved to an empty policy string for at least some eu-west tenants, and the retry code path treated an empty policy as a zero-millisecond delay instead of falling back to the configured default (config_retry_default="exponential"). Each affected job burned its entire retry budget in tens of milliseconds and was immediately re-queued, producing a self-sustaining hot retry loop.

The loop explains the otherwise contradictory observations:

  • CPU fell while the queue grew. Retry cycles completed in ~38–41 ms versus ~830 ms for a real successful job. Workers were cycling jobs, not completing them; time shifted from computation into pool wait (wait_ms=5000).
  • Scaling workers 40 → 90 made it worse. More workers meant more concurrent retries and more contention for a fixed 300-connection budget, so the queue grew faster.
  • The API rollback alone did not clear the backlog (09:24 → 09:43). The rollback fixed job creation; the retry execution behaviour was still governed by the still-enabled feature flag on the workers.
  • A worker restart helped for only ~4 minutes (09:31 → 09:35). worker-51 started with feature_flag=true, so a restart could only clear transient in-memory state; the zero-delay behaviour was re-established from config.
  • Disabling the flag produced recovery within two minutes. Post-disable logs show delay_ms=0 → delay_ms=1000 and job=319 succeeding, exactly the signature of exponential backoff being restored.

The mechanism is supported by a coherent pre/post comparison on the same job and worker. It is still an inference: we have one identified affected tenant (acme), one clean tenant (beta), and 13 log lines overall.

Contributing factors

  1. No safe fallback for an empty policy. The system had a correct default (exponential) available but did not use it when the tenant-specific lookup returned "".
  2. Retry budget with no floor on delay. A budget consumed in 41 ms provides no protection; there was no minimum backoff or per-tenant rate limit.
  3. Shared, fixed database connection pool (300). Retry amplification fed directly into pool exhaustion, coupling the queue to the API's ability to get connections.
  4. Rollout coupling without independent blast-radius control. The API deploy and the feature flag were separate controls, and the flag's exposure differed by region (US 2%), which is why the same release looked healthy in one region.
  5. Scaling was the first response. Worker count was raised on the assumption of insufficient capacity, which amplified rather than relieved the loop.
  6. Alerting lagged the onset by ~4 minutes (09:07 degradation → 09:11 alerts), and no alert fired on retry-budget exhaustion itself.

4. Hypotheses, with supporting and weakening evidence

H1 — Zero-delay retry loop caused by an empty tenant retry policy. ✅ Most likely

Supporting

  • policy="" appears in both retry_budget_exhausted events (09:06:58, 09:35:12).
  • Three attempts for job=81 occurred in the same second with delay_ms=0.
  • After the flag was disabled, the same job pattern changed to delay_ms=1000 and succeeded (09:43:09–10).
  • A non-affected tenant (beta) succeeded normally at 09:07:03, while acme failed — the failure tracks the tenant, not the worker or the database.
  • The dose–response signal: US had the flag on for 2% of tenants and did not alert.

Weakening

  • We have no log showing a job with an empty policy in the US region, so the 2%/no-alert datum is suggestive, not a controlled comparison.
  • We cannot see the code, so "empty policy ⇒ zero delay" is inferred from co-occurrence, not from reading the fallback logic.
  • Only two tenants are named; we do not know the true affected population.
  • The 4 minutes of normal processing after the 09:31 restart is not fully explained by this hypothesis and may point to an additional stateful element.

H2 — Database connection pool exhaustion was the primary cause. ⚠️ Amplifier, not trigger

Supporting

  • db pool wait exceeded wait_ms=5000 at 09:07:01.
  • Connections hit the 300 maximum by 09:20; API could not obtain slots.
  • Recovery followed the removal of retry pressure.

Weakening

  • Pool pressure was recorded after the retry anomaly (09:06:58) and at the same time as the queue rise (09:07), so it is more consistent with a consequence than with an origin.
  • No slow-query or database-error entries appear in the logs — the complaint is acquiring a connection, not the database being slow or down.
  • At 09:07:01 the pool showed active=10 idle=0 while waits exceeded 5 s. Ten active connections is far below a 300 ceiling, which does not fit simple saturation and is currently unexplained (see unknowns).
  • Recovery to a cleared backlog occurred without any stated change to pool size, which argues the pool was not the binding root constraint.

H3 — Insufficient worker capacity. ❌ Weak

Supporting

  • Queue depth was rising, which is what under-capacity looks like.

Weakening

  • Worker CPU fell from 65% to 18% — under-capacity presents with high CPU.
  • Increasing workers 40 → 90 made the queue rise faster. This directly contradicts the capacity hypothesis and is strong evidence against it.
  • One restarted pool processed normally for four minutes at the old behaviour set, showing headroom existed.

H4 — An underlying database or upstream dependency failure caused the retries. ⚠️ Possible, poorly supported

Supporting

  • Retries and pool waits are both consistent with a dependency problem.
  • A job can be retried for reasons unrelated to the retry policy itself.

Weakening

  • The observed retries carry delay_ms=0, which is a policy defect, not a dependency symptom. A dependency failure would still use normal backoff.
  • Another tenant on the same infrastructure succeeded normally at 09:07:03.
  • No database error, timeout, or 5xx entries appear in the logs.
  • Behaviour tracked the flag state across a disable, which points at the flag rather than at the dependency.

H5 — The API deploy was the root cause and the rollback resolved it. ⚠️ Partially correct

Supporting

  • Degradation began ~5 minutes after the 09:02 deploy.
  • Rolling back the deploy at 09:24 normalized new job creation.

Weakening

  • The backlog stayed stuck for a further 19 minutes after rollback and drained only after the flag was disabled at 09:43 — so the deploy was the delivery vector, while the flag governed the ongoing behaviour.
  • Workers that started after the rollback (09:31:10) still reproduced the fault, showing the rollback did not remove the faulty behaviour from the worker path.

5. What remains unknown

  1. Scope of the empty-policy condition. Which and how many tenants resolved to policy="", and whether the US 2% cohort included any of them.
  2. Why the policy was empty. We have no configuration diff, no flag evaluation records, and no visibility into how tenant policies are stored or propagated. Misconfiguration, a missing default, and a propagation failure all remain open.
  3. The active=10 idle=0 observation with 5-second waits. Whether this reflects a per-pool sub-limit, leaked or orphaned connections, connections held across long operations, or a logging artifact.
  4. Final job disposition. Whether jobs that exhausted their retry budget dead-lettered, were dropped, were duplicated, or were retried externally. The logs stop at retry_budget_exhausted.
  5. Customer-facing severity. Count of affected checkouts, peak and sustained latency, and failure-versus-slowdown split. Checkout latency alerted but no error-rate data is present.
  6. Blast radius beyond the queue. Whether the 300-connection saturation degraded the API, other worker pools, or other services sharing the database.
  7. The four-minute recovery after the 09:31 restart. What transient state the restart cleared, and whether it indicates a second contributing mechanism.
  8. Detection gap. Why recovery was only achieved at 09:43 when the link between the flag and worker retry behaviour was already observable in logs at 09:06:58.

hy4-preview/09-incident-analysis/TASK.md

Task 09 — Incident analysis

Analyze TIMELINE.md and LOGS.txt. Produce:

  • INCIDENT.md: impact, concise timeline, most likely root cause, contributing factors, evidence supporting or weakening each hypothesis, and what remains unknown.
  • ACTIONS.md: immediate containment plus prioritized corrective actions with owners expressed as roles, verification signals, and rollback considerations.
  • CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md: a plain-language update under 180 words. Do not claim certainty beyond the evidence or blame an individual.

Separate observed facts from inference. Avoid treating correlation as proof. Write RESPONSE.md with your confidence level and the next two pieces of data you would request. Work only in this directory.

Response — confidence and next data requests

Confidence

Overall: moderate. We are confident about what was observed; we are less confident about the precise mechanism.

Claim Confidence Basis
The incident began with, and was driven by, the tenant retry-policy change shipped in 2026.09.23.1 and gated by tenant_retry_policy Moderate–high Zero-delay retries and policy="" in the logs; failure persisted through a code rollback; recovery within seconds of the flag being disabled; US region at 2% exposure generated no alert (dose–response)
Affected tenants' policies resolved to an empty value and produced no backoff High delay_ms=0 on every attempt, policy="" in both exhaustion warnings, config_retry_default="exponential" not applied
Workers were blocked, not overloaded High Queue depth rising while worker CPU fell 65% → 18%; adding workers made the queue grow faster
The database connection pool was saturated and was the proximate stall Moderate Pool wait >5 s at 09:07:01, 300/300 by 09:20, reserved slots at 09:19:52 — but no DB-side data confirming what held the connections
The specific causal chain "zero-delay retries → connection saturation → worker starvation" Low–moderate Plausible and consistent with every observation, but the middle link is inferred; H2 (pool exhaustion as an independent cause) is not excluded
No customer data was lost Low Absence of evidence, not evidence of absence. retry_budget_exhausted jobs' final state is unknown

The strongest evidence — flag-off at 09:43:07 → delay_ms=1000 at 09:43:09 → success at 09:43:10 — is temporal coupling, which is suggestive but not proof. We have deliberately not attributed this to any individual; the observable facts point at a configuration/fallback defect in a change, not at a person.

Next two pieces of data I would request

  1. Database-side telemetry for 09:00–09:50 UTC: connection counts over time broken down by state (idle in transaction / active / waiting) and by holding client or query, plus lock-wait and top-query/slow-query data. Paired with worker-side wait-event or stack samples showing what the idle workers were blocked on during 09:07–09:43.

    • Why: this is the single missing link between the retry change and the stall. It discriminates H1 (retry storm saturating the pool) from H2 (independent pool exhaustion/leak) and explains why a restarted pool recovered for exactly four minutes.

  2. The resolved retry-policy configuration per tenant at the time of the incident, plus the flag rollout configuration (which tenants/regions had tenant_retry_policy enabled, at what percentage) and the job-level outcome records for every job that hit retry_budget_exhausted between 09:02 and 09:45 (dropped / dead-lettered / re-queued / eventually succeeded).

    • Why: this converts the two-tenant sample (acme failed, beta succeeded) into an actual blast radius, confirms whether the US region was truly unaffected or merely below alert thresholds, and determines whether any customer work was lost — the one impact question we cannot currently answer.

P3 — Detect and diagnose faster (this quarter)

A8 Alert on the "stalled worker" signature itself: queue depth rising while worker CPU/throughput falls. This fires earlier than job-age alerts did (09:07 vs 09:11). Observability owner + Queue service owner Alert reproduces against the recorded 09:07–09:11 data Alert-only
A9 Capture worker wait-event / stack-sample telemetry so that "why is the worker idle" is answerable from data next time. Queue service owner Idle-worker dashboards show blocking reason (connection wait vs lock vs sleep) Sampling overhead — enable at low rate, monitor worker overhead
A10 Add per-tenant throughput, retry-rate, and job-age dashboards so a 2%-exposure rollout produces a measurable signal. Observability owner Per-tenant metrics visible for a staging tenant at 1% traffic Metrics-only
A11 Update the incident response runbook: when scaling workers makes the queue worse, stop scaling and look for a shared-resource bottleneck. On-call engineer (SRE) lead Runbook section merged; reviewed in the incident retro Doc change only

3. Cross-cutting rollback considerations

  • The flag is the fastest lever. tenant_retry_policy off restored correct behaviour in seconds; keep this kill switch available and document it as the first response for any retry-behaviour regression.
  • Rollback ≠ revert. This incident's defining lesson: the code rollback was correct and complete, and still did not restore service, because behaviour was config/flag-driven. Every future rollback needs a behavioural assertion (A6), not just a version check.
  • Re-enabling the flag is the risky step. Sequence: fix (A1) → guardrail (A2) → observability (A3, A8) → staged rollout with auto-kill (A7) → 1% in one region → metric gate → ramp.
  • Connection-pool changes (A4) can throttle healthy traffic, so ship quotas with generous initial limits and tighten with data.
  • Any replay of affected jobs (C3) must be idempotency-checked first; a replay that double-executes side effects would be worse than the original delay.

4. Exit criteria for closing this incident

  1. Backlog cleared and job-age SLO met in all regions (met at 10:18 for eu-west — confirm sustained).
  2. Every retry_budget_exhausted job accounted for, with customer notification if any work was lost.
  3. A1, A2, A3 merged and verified in staging.
  4. Post-rollback behavioural verification (A6) exercised in a drill.

hy4-preview-codebuddy/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Service update — EU region delays, 23 September

Between 09:07 and 10:18 UTC on 23 September, customers in our EU region saw delays: queued jobs took far longer than usual and checkout was slower for a period. The backlog cleared by 10:18 UTC and service is operating normally.

What happened: a routine release changed how we retry work that fails on the first attempt. For some accounts the new setting was incomplete, so retries ran immediately instead of waiting. This put pressure on our database, leaving our workers waiting instead of processing.

What we did: we reverted the release, which restored normal handling of new work. The backlog stayed slow, so we also turned off the feature behind the new retry behaviour at 09:43 UTC. Processing recovered within minutes.

Where things stand: we have seen no sign of lost data, and other regions were largely unaffected. We are still confirming details, and are adding stricter retry limits and better protection for database capacity. Please contact support if you think your account was affected.

hy4-preview-codebuddy/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident analysis — eu-west queue backlog, 2026-09-23

Sources: TIMELINE.md, LOGS.txt (both provided; no other data available). Dates/times are UTC. Everything below is labelled observed (present in the sources) or inferred (our interpretation).

1. Summary

A release that introduced a tenant-specific retry policy was deployed at 09:02. Within five minutes, affected tenants' jobs began retrying with a zero delay and exhausting their retry budget in ~40 ms. Queue depth in eu-west rose from 09:07 and worker CPU fell (65% → 18%), i.e. workers stopped making progress rather than being overloaded. Database connections saturated (300/300 by 09:20). Rolling back the API deploy at 09:24 restored new job creation but did not clear the backlog, because the behaviour was gated by the feature flag tenant_retry_policy, not by the deployed code. Disabling that flag at 09:43 restored correct backoff within seconds; the backlog cleared by 10:18.

2. Impact (observed unless marked otherwise)

  • Region: eu-west. US ran the same release but with the flag enabled for only 2% of tenants; no alert fired there (observed).
  • Window: first degradation 09:07 → backlog cleared 10:18 (~71 min). Mitigation took effect 09:43, so ~36 min of degraded processing after the first remediation attempt and ~4 min from the effective fix to the start of recovery.
  • Customer-visible symptoms: job age SLO breach and checkout latency alerts (observed, 09:11). Processing of queued jobs stalled; new job creation was also affected until 09:24 (observed: "new job creation normalizes" after the rollback).
  • Blast radius: unknown. The number of jobs delayed, the number of tenants affected, and the worst-case job age are not in the evidence.
  • Job outcomes: logs show retry_budget_exhausted for job=81 and job=319. Whether those jobs were dropped, dead-lettered, or re-queued is unknown — so we cannot yet state whether any customer work was lost. No evidence of data corruption is present in the logs.
  • Severity framing (inferred): a full regional processing stall lasting a little over an hour, with customer-visible checkout latency.

3. Timeline (observed)

Time (UTC) Observation Source
09:02 Deploy of api 2026.09.23.1; change adds tenant-specific retry policy. TIMELINE
09:06:58 worker-17: job=81 tenant=acme, attempts 1–3 all result=retry delay_ms=0, then retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=41. LOGS
09:07:01 worker-22: db pool wait exceeded wait_ms=5000 active=10 idle=0. LOGS
09:07:03 worker-08: job=92 tenant=beta, attempt=1 result=success elapsed_ms=830 (a different tenant succeeds). LOGS
09:07 Queue depth begins rising in eu-west; worker CPU falls 65% → 18%. TIMELINE
09:11 Alerts fire for job age and checkout latency. TIMELINE
09:14 On-call scales workers 40 → 90; queue depth rises faster. TIMELINE
09:19:52 api-03: db remaining connection slots reserved. LOGS
09:20 Database connections reach the configured maximum of 300. TIMELINE
09:24 API deploy rolled back; new job creation normalizes; backlog still stuck. TIMELINE
09:31:10 worker-51 startup: config_retry_default="exponential" feature_flag=true. LOGS
09:31 One worker pool restarted; processes jobs normally for four minutes, then stalls. TIMELINE
09:35:12 worker-51: job=319 retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=38 — same failure mode as 09:06, i.e. still present after rollback. LOGS
09:43:07 worker-51: config_changed feature_flag=false. LOGS
09:43:09 worker-51: job=319 attempt=1 result=retry delay_ms=1000 (backoff restored). LOGS
09:43:10 worker-51: job=319 attempt=2 result=success elapsed_ms=190. LOGS
~09:45 Recovery begins (within two minutes of flag disable). TIMELINE
10:18 Backlog cleared. TIMELINE

4. Observed facts vs. inference

Observed

  1. Retries for tenant acme executed with delay_ms=0 on all three attempts.
  2. Both exhaustion warnings carry policy="" — the resolved policy was an empty string.
  3. Total elapsed time to exhaust the budget was ~40 ms (41 ms, 38 ms).
  4. Worker CPU dropped while queue depth rose.
  5. DB pool wait exceeded 5 s as early as 09:07:01, before any scaling.
  6. DB connections hit the 300 maximum at 09:20; api-03 reported reserved slots at 09:19:52.
  7. Scaling 40 → 90 workers made queue depth rise faster.
  8. Code rollback at 09:24 fixed job creation but not the backlog.
  9. A restarted pool recovered for ~4 minutes, then stalled.
  10. Disabling tenant_retry_policy restored delay_ms=1000 backoff and successes within ~3 seconds; recovery followed within ~2 minutes.
  11. US had the same release, 2% flag exposure, no alert.

Inferred

  1. The empty policy meant "no backoff" rather than "use the default" — with the flag on, config_retry_default="exponential" was not applied (worker-51 logged that default at startup, yet still hit policy="").
  2. Zero-delay retries multiplied work per job (tight retry loop and/or rapid re-enqueue), increasing DB work and connection hold time per job.
  3. That drove connection-pool saturation, which left workers blocked waiting for connections — the most consistent explanation for rising queue with falling CPU, and for more workers making things worse.
  4. The four-minute post-restart stall reflects a resource that re-accumulates (connections/locks), not a fixed code path that fails immediately.
  5. Recovery at flag-disable is strong but not conclusive causal evidence: the flag change and the recovery are tightly coupled in time, and the US dose-response (2% exposure → no alert) is consistent with a causal role.

5. Most likely root cause

The tenant-specific retry policy shipped in 2026.09.23.1 resolved to an empty policy (policy="") for affected tenants and was then treated as "retry immediately with no backoff" instead of falling back to the configured default (config_retry_default="exponential"). Affected jobs burned their entire retry budget in ~40 ms, producing a retry/re-enqueue storm that saturated the shared database connection pool (300/300 by 09:20). Workers then blocked on connection acquisition — hence rising queue depth with falling CPU — and the incident persisted until the flag was disabled and backoff (delay_ms=1000) returned.

Why this ranks first: it is the only hypothesis that explains (a) the zero-delay retries and policy="" in the logs, (b) the failure persisting through a code rollback, (c) the near-instant recovery at flag-disable, and (d) the US region showing no alert at 2% exposure.

Confidence: moderate-to-high that the flag-gated retry policy triggered the incident; moderate that DB connection saturation is the specific mechanism that stalled the workers (see H2 and the unknowns).

6. Hypotheses, with evidence for and against

# Hypothesis Evidence supporting Evidence weakening / contra
H1 Empty tenant retry policy → zero-delay retry storm → downstream saturation (most likely) delay_ms=0 x3 then retry_budget_exhausted policy="" (09:06:58, 09:35:12); recovery within seconds of feature_flag=false (09:43:07–09:43:10); failure persisted through code rollback; US 2% exposure → no alert No log line shows increased DB work per job directly; the causal chain from zero-delay retries to pool saturation is inferred, not measured
H2 DB connection-pool exhaustion / connection leak as an independent primary cause db pool wait exceeded wait_ms=5000 active=10 idle=0 at 09:07:01; 300/300 at 09:20; api-03 reserved slots at 09:19:52; adding workers made it worse; restart gave ~4 min of relief Doesn't explain why disabling a retry-policy flag resolved it within ~2 min, nor the policy=""/delay_ms=0 pattern; a pure leak in long-lived code would not clear that fast
H3 Insufficient worker capacity On-call's initial read; queue depth rising Weak: CPU fell to 18% (workers idle, not saturated), and increasing workers 40 → 90 made the queue grow faster
H4 Faulty API-side change in job creation only Deploy at 09:02 preceded degradation at 09:07 Weak: after rollback at 09:24 job creation normalized but the backlog stayed stuck, so the consumption path was also affected
H5 Rollback was incomplete (config/flag not reverted) Backlog stuck after rollback; worker-51 started at 09:31:10 with feature_flag=true and reproduced policy="" at 09:35 This is a contributing factor, not a root cause: it explains why remediation #1 failed, not why the fault started
H6 Lock contention / slow queries from an unrelated source (e.g. migration, batch job) Would also explain falling CPU with idle workers No such evidence anywhere in the provided material; timing lines up with the deploy, but this cannot be excluded without DB telemetry
H7 Tenant-specific config lookup miss (only some tenants have a policy defined) acme failed, beta succeeded at 09:07:03; policy resolved to "" Consistent with H1 rather than competing — it is the likely mechanism inside H1, but which tenants lacked a policy is unknown

Correlation caution: the deploy, the flag, and the DB saturation all co-occur. The tightest causal signal is the flag-disable → recovery pair, and even that could in principle be coincidence or reflect a concurrently decaying backlog. We treat H1 as most likely, not proven.

7. Contributing factors

  1. Behaviour gated by a feature flag, not by the deploy. The rollback at 09:24 reverted code while the flag stayed on, so the first remediation did not work and ~19 minutes of additional impact accrued.
  2. Empty/incomplete configuration accepted as valid. policy="" was not rejected and did not fall back to the configured exponential default; delay_ms=0 was accepted as a retry delay.
  3. No guardrail against zero/immediate retry. Nothing bounded retry rate at the worker or tenant level.
  4. Shared, un-isolated database connection pool. One pool served all tenants, so retry amplification in a subset of tenants starved everyone; no per-tenant quota is evident.
  5. Capacity response amplified the fault. Scaling 40 → 90 workers added connection demand onto an already saturated shared resource.
  6. Staged flag exposure did not produce an early signal. US ran the same release at 2% exposure and stayed below alert thresholds — insufficient exposure (or insufficiently sensitive metrics) to catch the fault pre-scale.
  7. Detection and diagnosis lag. Alerts fired ~4 min after queue depth began rising; the effective fix came ~36 min after first degradation.

8. What remains unknown

  • The resolved retry-policy value per tenant, and which tenants were affected (only acme failing and beta succeeding is observed — a sample of two).
  • Whether policy="" came from a missing tenant config, a serialization/lookup bug, or a bad default — and why the exponential default did not apply.
  • DB-side truth: connection counts by state and holder, lock waits, slow queries, whether connections were leaked or merely busy.
  • Why worker CPU fell: whether workers blocked on connection acquisition, on locks, or on something else. No stack samples or wait-event data available.
  • Job-level outcomes: how many jobs were delayed; whether any retry_budget_exhausted jobs were dropped, dead-lettered, or re-queued; peak job age; SLA credits implications.
  • Whether the 09:31 pool restart helped because of fresh connections (favouring H2) or because of a transient config/cache state (favouring H1).
  • Whether US was genuinely unaffected or merely below alerting thresholds.
  • Whether the same failure mode can recur for a tenant whose policy is unset, independent of the flag.

muse/09-incident-analysis/RESPONSE.md

Response note

Confidence: medium-high (not certain)

I judge the flag-gated empty retry policy (policy="" → delay_ms=0) causing retry-driven database saturation to be the most likely cause, but correlation is not proof. The timing (onset after deploy, recovery within ~2 minutes of flag disable), the same job switching from zero-delay exhaustion to 1,000 ms backoff plus success across the flag flip, the restart-then-restall pattern while the flag stayed on, and the US region running the same release at 2% flag exposure with no alert all point the same way. Against certainty: the log sample is tiny, policy="" is unexplained without code/config, aggregate retry and tenant distributions are missing, and DB topology is unknown, so an independent DB-side contribution cannot be fully excluded.

Next two pieces of data I would request

  1. Full worker retry telemetry plus the policy-resolution code and configuration: aggregate delay_ms, retry_budget_exhausted, and success/failure counts by tenant and time (09:00–10:30, eu-west vs. US), with the code path that maps a tenant to policy="" and then to delay_ms=0, and the flag's rollout history per region/tenant. This would confirm or refute the empty-policy mechanism and show whether one tenant or all tenants drove the storm.
  2. Database and queue resource time series with topology: DB connections, pool waits, active/idle time, queries and slow queries by source (API vs. workers) overlaid with queue depth, worker count, and worker CPU, plus whether eu-west and US share a database and the per-service connection budgets. This would establish whether DB saturation was the mediating bottleneck and why scaling workers made the queue rise faster.

muse/09-incident-analysis/ACTIONS.md

Corrective actions — eu-west queue stall 2026-09-23

Owner convention: roles, not individuals.

Immediate containment (already taken — verify they hold)

  1. Keep tenant_retry_policy disabled in all regions until the fix is validated.
    • Owner: Release Manager / Feature Flag Owner.
    • Verification: flag state reads false everywhere; worker logs show no policy="" and no delay_ms=0; retry_budget_exhausted rate at baseline.
    • Rollback: re-enabling is the rollback of this containment — do not re-enable except under the staged rollout below.
  2. Confirm full recovery and job disposition.
    • Owner: On-call / SRE.
    • Verification: queue depth and job age at baseline since 10:18 with no rebound; worker CPU back to ~65%; DB connections well below 300 with no pool-wait warnings; audit dead-letter/dropped jobs from 09:07–10:18 and replay or notify as policy requires.
    • Rollback: n/a (observational).
  3. Freeze related deploys and flag changes until reviewed.
    • Owner: Incident Commander.
    • Verification: deploy/flag audit log shows no retry-policy changes.
    • Rollback: lift freeze explicitly after P0 fixes land.

Prioritized corrective actions

P0 — Fix unsafe retry fallback (root-cause fix)

  • Action: when tenant policy is missing/empty/unparseable, fall back to the configured default (exponential) instead of zero delay; reject delay_ms=0 with a validated minimum backoff plus jitter; add unit + integration tests for empty/missing/legacy tenant configs.
    • Owner: Backend / Worker Service Owner.
    • Verification: tests cover policy="", missing tenant, malformed policy; staging chaos test shows delay_ms >= floor always; canary shows zero policy="" / delay_ms=0 events.
    • Rollback: revert to prior worker build and keep flag off; rollback signal is any recurrence of delay_ms=0 or retry_budget_exhausted spike on canary.

P0 — Cap retry amplification

  • Action: enforce per-job minimum delay, per-worker retry rate limit, and a time-based retry budget (not just attempt count); add a circuit breaker that pauses retries for a tenant/worker when exhaustion rate spikes.
    • Owner: Backend / Worker Service Owner.
    • Verification: load test with forced downstream errors shows bounded retry rate and stable DB connections; breaker trips and recovers in staging.
    • Rollback: disable breaker via flag if it false-trips; keep minimum-delay floor in place (do not roll back to zero-delay behavior).

P0 — Protect the database bottleneck

  • Action: split and cap connection budgets (API vs. workers, per pool) so workers cannot starve the API and vice versa; set per-worker pool max consistent with DB max (e.g., total possible < 300 with headroom); add pool-wait and connection-use alerts.
    • Owner: Database Reliability Engineer with SRE.
    • Verification: worst-case connection math documented; saturation test shows graceful queueing with alerts before errors; remaining connection slots never recurs below declared capacity.
    • Rollback: restore prior pool settings if throughput regresses; change one pool at a time during low traffic.

P1 — Safe scaling and backpressure

  • Action: block worker scale-up when pool waits or DB saturation are active; scale on job age only when downstream headroom exists; document "do not scale into saturation" in the runbook.
    • Owner: SRE.
    • Verification: game-day replay of 09:14 shows autoscaler/runbook refusing scale-up and paging instead.
    • Rollback: revert autoscaler policy change; manual scaling remains available.

P1 — Staged flag rollout with auto-halt

  • Action: roll out tenant_retry_policy progressively (e.g., 1% → 10% → 50% → 100% per region) with automatic halt/rollback on guardrails: delay_ms=0 count, retry_budget_exhausted rate, queue age, worker CPU drop, DB waits.
    • Owner: Release Manager / Feature Flag Owner with SRE.
    • Verification: staged rollout in US first with halt drill; eu-west re-enable only after P0 fixes and soak period.
    • Rollback: one-click flag disable; pre-tested kill switch.

P1 — Detection and alerting gaps

  • Action: alert on leading indicators, not just lagging job age: rate of retry_budget_exhausted, any delay_ms=0, worker CPU drop concurrent with queue rise, DB pool-wait p99, connection utilization >80%.
    • Owner: SRE / Observability Owner.
    • Verification: replay 09:06–09:11 logs against new rules — alert would have fired by ~09:07.
    • Rollback: tune thresholds if noisy; do not delete without replacement.

P2 — Post-incident hygiene

  • Action: publish incident review; reconcile dead-lettered jobs; add a worker/DB saturation load test to CI; separate API-deploy rollback from worker/flag state in runbooks.
    • Owner: Incident Commander (review); Backend Owner (tests); SRE (runbooks).
    • Verification: review approved; job audit complete; new load test passing.
    • Rollback: n/a.

muse/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Customer update — checkout and job delays in eu-west

On September 23, between about 09:07 and 10:18 UTC, some customers in eu-west saw slow checkouts and delayed background jobs. Our systems alerted us at 09:11, and the backlog was fully cleared by 10:18. Customers in the US were not affected.

What happened, as far as we can tell so far: after a software change, some jobs began retrying too aggressively, which overloaded a shared database and slowed processing. Scaling up did not help at first. Service began recovering shortly after we turned off the related feature, and then returned to normal.

We are still investigating the full cause and checking whether any jobs need to be replayed. Next, we are adding safer retry defaults, better overload protection, and earlier alerts. We will share another update once our review is complete. Thank you for your patience.

muse/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident report — eu-west job queue stall — 2026-09-23

Convention in this file: Observed = directly stated in TIMELINE.md / LOGS.txt. Inference = interpretation; labeled as such.

Impact (observed + bounds)

Observed:

  • Region affected: eu-west. US region had no alert (same release, flag at 2% of tenants).
  • Queue depth began rising ~09:07 UTC; backlog cleared 10:18 UTC (~71 minutes of degraded queueing).
  • Alerts fired 09:11 for job age and checkout latency — checkout path was user-impacted.
  • Worker CPU fell 65% → 18% while queue rose: workers were stalled/blocked, not keeping up.
  • Database connections reached configured maximum (300) at 09:20; API logged remaining connection slots reserved at 09:19:52; workers logged DB pool waits (5,000 ms, 0 idle).
  • Some jobs still succeeded during the incident (e.g., job 92, tenant beta, 09:07:03, 830 ms), so impact was partial, not a full stop — exact success/failure split is unknown.

Unknown (not in evidence): number of delayed/failed jobs, delay distribution, number of affected tenants/customers, checkout latency magnitude or failed checkouts, whether exhausted jobs were lost or later succeeded, any data-integrity effects.

Concise timeline (observed, UTC)

  • 09:02 — API 2026.09.23.1 deployed; change adds tenant-specific retry policy.
  • 09:06:58 — worker-17 logs job 81 (tenant acme): 3 retry attempts with delay_ms=0 in the same second, then retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=41.
  • 09:07 — Queue depth rises (eu-west); worker CPU 65% → 18%.
  • 09:07:01 — worker-22: DB pool wait exceeded (5 s, active=10, idle=0).
  • 09:11 — Alerts: job age, checkout latency.
  • 09:14 — Workers scaled 40 → 90 (inference: manual mitigation); queue then rises faster.
  • 09:19–09:20 — DB contention: API connection-slot error; DB connections at max 300.
  • 09:24 — API deploy rolled back; new job creation normalizes, backlog stays stuck.
  • 09:31 — One worker pool restarted; processes normally ~4 minutes, then stalls. Restarted worker shows config_retry_default="exponential" feature_flag=true.
  • 09:35:12 — Same restarted worker logs retry_budget_exhausted policy="" again.
  • 09:43:07 — Feature flag tenant_retry_policy disabled; worker logs config_changed feature_flag=false.
  • 09:43:09–09:43:10 — Same job 319: retry with delay_ms=1000, then success (190 ms). Recovery begins within ~2 minutes.
  • 10:18 — Backlog cleared.
  • US: same release, flag at 2% of tenants, no alert.

Most likely root cause (inference, medium-high confidence)

Enabling tenant_retry_policy caused affected workers to resolve an empty retry policy (policy="") with zero backoff (delay_ms=0), producing rapid retries that exhausted the per-job retry budget in ~40 ms. The resulting retry amplification plausibly saturated the database connection pool (300 max), causing workers to block on pool waits (CPU drop, queue growth). Scaling workers increased DB demand and worsened the stall. Disabling the flag restored the default exponential backoff (1,000 ms delay observed) and recovery followed within minutes.

This is the best-fit inference, not proven causation: timing is correlational, the log sample is small, and the code path resolving policy="" was not examined. See hypotheses and unknowns below.

Contributing factors (inference grounded in observations)

  1. Unsafe fallback: empty/missing tenant policy appears to yield zero delay instead of the configured exponential default.
  2. No retry floor: delay_ms=0 retries with no minimum backoff or jitter allowed a tight retry burst.
  3. Shared, capped downstream: DB max 300 connections became the bottleneck; API and workers contend for the same slots (both logged saturation).
  4. Scale-up without backpressure: adding workers (40 → 90) during pool-wait/connection saturation increased contention; queue rose faster afterward.
  5. Flag scoping: eu-west appears broadly exposed while US was at 2% — no progressive rollout or auto-halt on queue/CPU/DB signals is evident.
  6. Split mitigation surface: API rollback (09:24) did not change worker/flag behavior, so the backlog stayed stuck; worker restart helped only transiently (~4 min).

Hypotheses — evidence for and against

H1 — Flag-gated empty retry policy → retry storm → DB exhaustion → worker stall (favored)

Supporting (observed):

  • delay_ms=0 × 3 + retry_budget_exhausted policy="" in <1 s, twice (09:06:58, 09:35:12), both while flag was on.
  • After feature_flag=false, same job shows delay_ms=1000 then success — behavior change coincides with flag change on the same worker.
  • Recovery begins within ~2 min of flag disable; backlog clears by 10:18.
  • Worker restart with feature_flag=true stalls again after ~4 min — consistent with a flag-dependent load trigger, not a one-time poisoned state.
  • US on same release with 2% flag exposure had no alert — weakens "release alone" and is consistent with "flag exposure dose matters."
  • CPU fall + pool waits + connection max + worse-after-scale-up match downstream-saturation mechanics.

Weakening / missing:

  • Small log sample (a handful of jobs); no aggregate retry-rate or per-tenant failure distribution.
  • No code/config showing how policy="" arises or that zero-delay retries issue DB load per attempt.
  • CPU drop is consistent with blocking but does not by itself prove DB was the first bottleneck.

H2 — The API release itself (bad job creation / poison jobs), independent of the flag

Supporting:

  • Onset ~5 min after deploy (09:02 → 09:07) — temporal correlation.

Weakening:

  • API rollback (09:24) normalized new job creation but did not unstick the backlog; recovery tracked flag disable (09:43), not rollback.
  • US ran the same release with no alert.
  • Retried-then-succeeded behavior on the same job across the flag flip points to worker retry handling, not permanently poisoned job payloads.

H3 — Independent database failure or capacity regression (not caused by retries)

Supporting:

  • DB saturation is directly observed (pool waits, slot errors, 300/300).

Weakening:

  • No DB change is in the timeline; saturation onset tracks the deploy/flag window, and recovery tracks flag disable with no recorded DB intervention.
  • Restart-then-restall and US-no-alert patterns are unexplained by a purely DB-side fault unless DBs are regionally isolated (unknown — see below).

H4 — Worker leak / resource exhaustion unrelated to retry logic

Supporting:

  • Restart produced ~4 min of normal processing — consistent with clearing leaked/accumulated state.

Weakening:

  • Re-stall in 4 min is fast for a classic slow leak and matches re-saturation under continued bad retry load; permanent recovery required flag disable, not repeated restarts.

H5 — Single-tenant poison (e.g., only acme)

Supporting:

  • The earliest failing job is tenant acme while tenant beta succeeds once at 09:07:03.

Weakening:

  • Broad queue/DB/checkout impact and a second exhausted job (319) are inconsistent with one tenant unless acme dominates load (tenant mix unknown). policy="" on multiple jobs suggests a systemic fallback, not one bad tenant row.

What remains unknown

  • What policy="" means (missing tenant row? parse failure? unmigrated default?) and the exact code path from policy to delay_ms=0.
  • Aggregate blast radius: jobs delayed/failed vs. succeeded, per-tenant distribution, checkout latency/error counts, customer count.
  • Final disposition of budget-exhausted jobs: dropped, dead-lettered, or retried to success after 09:43.
  • DB topology: shared vs. regional; per-service connection split (API vs. workers); queries per retry attempt; what held connections (idle-in-transaction? slow queries?).
  • Worker topology: pools, threads, and pool sizing (log shows active=10/worker — 40–90 workers × 10 would oversubscribe 300 even before retries).
  • Flag rollout history: when and to whom tenant_retry_policy was enabled in eu-west vs. US; who/what set it to 2% in US.
  • Why new job creation normalized after API rollback while backlog stayed stuck (separate API vs. worker flag reads? cached policy?).
  • Whether 09:24–09:43 backlog processing was zero or merely slow; queue-depth numbers over time.

09:43:07 worker-51 INFO config_changed feature_flag=false
09:43:09 worker-51 INFO job=319 attempt=1 result=retry delay_ms=1000
09:43:10 worker-51 INFO job=319 attempt=2 result=success elapsed_ms=190

Evaluation 7.75 / 10 graded blind as submission D

The most elaborate submission, with a rich causal chain, a rejected hot-CPU-loop hypothesis, and the sharp point that the pool wait at active=10 is worker-local. But it labels several unsupported claims FACT (100% eu-west exposure, retries continuing past budget exhaustion) and calls worker-state corruption 'ruled out'. Actions are excellent; customer update states inferred mechanism as fact and says impact was limited to Europe.

Strengths

  • H6 rejects a runaway CPU loop because CPU fell (INCIDENT.md:214-216)
  • Identifies active=10 idle=0 at 09:07:01 as a worker-local constraint far below the 300 cap
  • Flags the 5-minute deploy-to-failure and 4-minute restart-to-failure delays as an unexplained recurring trigger
  • Actions: P0-4 job-outcome audit, behavioural rollback verification, explicit re-enable sequencing

Weaknesses

  • INCIDENT.md:127 '100% exposure in eu-west ... (FACT)' - exposure is not in the sources
  • INCIDENT.md:124 'Retries continued past retry_budget_exhausted (FACT...)' - not shown in logs
  • Renames the US region 'us-east' and calls recovery at 09:43 'immediate and complete' though backlog lasted to 10:18
  • Customer update: 'For some accounts, no retry setting was found' as fact and 'The effect was limited to our European region', contradicting its own RESPONSE caveat
Evidence the grader checked
  • D/INCIDENT.md:124-132 FACT labels on unsupported claims
