mcode-m3.1-flash/09-incident-analysis/RESPONSE.md

RESPONSE.md

Confidence

Overall: moderate-to-high for the causal chain, low for the specific defect.

The evidence base is two files totalling roughly 30 lines. That is enough to reconstruct what happened with reasonable confidence, and not enough to pin the specific code or config defect. I have split the confidence deliberately rather than giving a single number, because the two halves are genuinely different in strength.

High confidence (directly observed, or falsified by a failed test):

  • An unresolved retry policy produced zero backoff. policy="" with delay_ms=0 on all three attempts, and the same worker/job pair flipping to delay_ms=1000 two seconds after the flag was disabled. This is the closest thing to a controlled comparison available.
  • Disabling tenant_retry_policy ended the incident; the backlog drained in 35 minutes.
  • The retry storm preceded the queue growth, not the other way round: 09:06:58 vs 09:07.
  • The API binary was not the whole cause. Its 09:24 rollback failed to recover service for 19 minutes. This is a real falsification, not a correlation.
  • Corrupted worker state is ruled out. A freshly restarted pool worked normally for four minutes, then reproduced the identical policy="" failure with a correct config_retry_default="exponential". Note what that simultaneously proves: the default was healthy, so the fault is in the tenant-specific override path.
  • A runaway CPU loop is ruled out. CPU fell 65% → 18%; workers were blocked on I/O.
  • Workers were starving before the API was (09:07:01 vs 09:19:52), and pure database capacity is ruled out as root cause — the 300-connection cap was not reached until 13 minutes after degradation began.

Moderate confidence (inference, plausible, not proven):

  • That zero-delay retries consumed connections by rate rather than through a connection leak on the failure path. Both produce the same symptoms; the inputs contain no connection-lifecycle data at all. This is the weakest load-bearing link in the chain and the reason I request metric data below.
  • That the 40 → 90 scale-up amplified rather than caused the incident. Cause is cleanly excluded on timing. Amplification is likely but confounded: queue depth is (arrival rate − service rate), so faster growth could partly reflect rising arrivals.
  • That us-east saw no impact because exposure was 2%. I would only claim the weaker statement: no alert fired there, which is not evidence of no impact. 2% exposure may simply sit under the alert threshold.

Low confidence / unresolved:

  • Whether the fault is a code defect or a bad config value (H5). The two readings predict identical evidence so far and I am explicitly not claiming the code is at fault over the configuration.
  • The trigger condition. First failure came ~5 min after deploy, and ~4 min after a worker restart. Something recurring provokes it, and I do not know what.
  • Customer impact magnitude, and whether any job failed permanently, was dropped, or ran twice. Entirely absent from the inputs. I have not assumed "no data loss" — absence of evidence here is not evidence of absence.
  • Whether the "new job creation normalizes" at 09:24 was caused by the rollback or coincided with it. Single region, no control.

Bounded by data access: this analysis is based only on TIMELINE.md and LOGS.txt. No external systems, logs, metrics, or configuration were consulted, and the logs are a 13-line sample, not a complete record — absence of a signal in this sample is weak evidence about the system.

The next two pieces of data I would request

1. The tenant retry-policy resolver diff for 2026.09.23.1, together with the per-tenant policy map as it actually stood at 09:02.

Specifically: the diff of the policy resolution path, and the config store's version history showing the full map contents at deploy time — including the entry for tenant acme and at least one tenant with no entry, plus the beta tenant for contrast against the one logged success at 09:07:03.

Why this is first: it is the only request that can resolve H5, which is the difference between a code fix and a config fix. It distinguishes a missing map entry, a tenant-key mismatch, a parse failure, and an error swallowed into an empty string — and it tells me whether the defect is reachable for any tenant or only for tenants with partial configuration. It also directly tests whether the tenant boundary (acme failing while beta succeeded) is load-bearing or coincidental. Nothing else in my analysis can substitute for it, and most of the P0 actions in ACTIONS.md change shape depending on the answer.

2. A 09:00–10:20 UTC time series for both eu-west and us-east: per-tenant retry attempt rate, database connection acquisition rate and hold duration, per-worker pool size against the global connection gauge, and the pool-wait histogram.

Why this is second: it is the only way to close the weakest link in the chain — whether connections were exhausted by retry rate or by a leak. It resolves the puzzle that worker-22 reported pool wait exceeded at active=10 idle=0 while the global cap of 300 was still 13 minutes away, which implies a worker-local constraint that is currently undocumented. It quantifies what the 40 → 90 scale-up actually added on top of the existing load, and comparing against us-east at 2% exposure is the only control available to test whether eu-west behaved differently for reasons other than exposure. It also gives the missing impact numbers — affected tenant count, retry volume, and how much of the delay each job absorbed — which is what a defensible customer-facing impact statement requires.

Between them, these two would move the specific defect from low confidence to high, and would let me put a real number on the affected population instead of an unquantified region.

mcode-m3.1-flash/09-incident-analysis/ACTIONS.md

ACTIONS.md — Corrective actions

Owners are roles, not individuals. Priorities are by risk reduction per unit of effort, not by effort alone. Every action carries a verification signal that is observable without the author's judgement, and a rollback note — a corrective action that cannot be independently reverted is itself a new risk.

Derived from INCIDENT.md. Root cause accepted at the level of chain, not specific defect, so every fix below must hold regardless of which of H5's two readings is correct.

Part 1 — Immediate containment (state at end of incident)

The incident is closed out: backlog cleared at 10:18. These are the containment positions to hold while Part 2 lands.

# Containment action Role Status
C1 Keep tenant_retry_policy disabled in all regions. Do not re-enable until P0-1 and P0-3 are verified in a canary. Release engineering In effect since 09:43 — hold
C2 Pin the queue service to release 2026.09.23.1-pre retry code. Verify no tenant anywhere still has the flag on, including eu-west stragglers. Queue service backend Verify, do not assume
C3 Freeze deploys and worker-count changes to the queue service until the P0s are complete. SRE on-call lead New
C4 Return eu-west worker count to the pre-incident 40 until the connection-budget change (P1-1) lands, and confirm the queue is stable at that count. SRE on-call lead New
C5 Publish the incident to the on-call channel with a pointer to INCIDENT.md, and state explicitly that the 09:24 rollback was not an effective mitigation. Incident commander New

C2 caveat: the 09:24 API rollback left the system broken for 19 minutes, so a rollback alone is not evidence of a reverted state. C2 requires a positive check (resolved policy non-empty, backoff non-zero on a synthetic retry), not a version number.

Part 2 — Prioritized corrective actions

P0 — before any re-enablement

P0-1 · Make an unresolved retry policy fall back to the safe default, never to zero backoff

  • Action: in the tenant retry-policy path, treat "policy not found / empty / unparseable" as a resolution failure and apply config_retry_default (already exponential and demonstrably correct in production at 09:31:10). Never allow a resolved policy to yield delay_ms=0. Add a hard floor on first-retry backoff independent of any policy value.
  • Owner: Queue service backend
  • Verification signal: unit test asserting that a tenant with no policy entry resolves to exponential with delay_ms >= floor; staging run with the flag on for a synthetic tenant produces delay_ms > 0 on first retry; metric retry_resolve_fallback_total increments instead of the policy silently resolving empty.
  • Rollback: single revertible commit; the feature flag remains an independent kill switch, so reverting the code while the flag is off is a no-op. Must be reverted alone, never bundled with another change.

P0-2 · Enforce a global retry ceiling and a circuit breaker on the DB path

  • Action: cap retry attempts globally and per tenant so no tenant's failure pattern can monopolize shared connections. Add a breaker that trips on pool-wait pressure. Make retry_budget_exhausted actually stop the loop — dead-letter or park the job, and record the disposition.
  • Owner: Platform reliability, with Queue service backend
  • Verification signal: fault-injection in staging — a tenant whose jobs fail 100% of the time — leaves eu-wide retry rate under the configured ceiling, DB pool wait p99 below threshold, and healthy tenants' p99 checkout latency unchanged. Dead-letter counts are non-zero and match injected failures.
  • Rollback: thresholds are config-driven behind a flag; revert to the previous threshold set without a deploy. Verify the previous values are still known-good — do not rely on "git revert the whole service".

P0-3 · Validate retry configuration at load time and refuse to start on an invalid value

  • Action: schema-validate the tenant policy map at worker startup. Reject or explicitly fall back on empty values, and log loudly rather than accepting "". A worker should never start in a state where its own policy is empty.
  • Owner: Configuration / config-management, with Queue service backend
  • Verification signal: staging worker started with an empty policy entry either refuses startup or starts on the documented default and increments retry_policy_empty_total; no worker in staging or production ever logs policy="" again.
  • Rollback: validation strictness is toggleable; keep the toggle and its current value documented. Note the failure mode to avoid — making validation so strict that a malformed unrelated entry blocks all workers, which converts a degraded queue into a stopped one.

P0-4 · Audit job outcomes from the incident window

  • Action: determine the final disposition of every job processed 09:07–10:18: succeeded, permanently failed, dead-lettered, dropped, duplicated, or executed more than once. Reconcile against checkout and payment totals. Contact affected customers; remediate duplicate or failed charges.
  • Owner: Support engineering, with Data engineering
  • Verification signal: audit returns a count per disposition class for the window; checkout and payment totals reconcile against the job ledger; a named owner confirms customer contact was made for every discrepancy found.
  • Rollback: none needed — read-only analysis plus customer remediation. If the audit is inconclusive, record that explicitly rather than assuming no impact.
  • Note: this is P0 despite being non-technical, because the inputs contain no evidence about job disposition, and permanent job failure is the failure mode that would actually hurt a customer.

P1 — before restoring full traffic

P1-1 · Make worker count and DB connection budget one coordinated setting

  • Worker pool sizing, per-worker pool size, and the global 300-connection cap must be derived from a single documented budget. worker-22 showed pool-wait exhaustion at active=10 idle=0 while global usage was far from 300, so the worker-local budget is the binding constraint and is currently undocumented.
  • Owner: Database / platform
  • Verification signal: load test at the 90-worker configuration keeps global connection usage below the cap with stated headroom and pool-wait p99 under 100 ms; a written "max safe workers" number exists in the runbook.
  • Rollback: the ratio is config-driven; restore the prior ratio independently. Reduce rather than remove workers, and watch the queue while doing it.

P1-2 · Require a canary and an automated rollback gate for flag-gated releases

  • Flags default off. No region or tenant cohort reaches full exposure without a canary step. Automated rollback triggers on retry rate, empty-policy resolution, and pool wait.
  • Owner: Release engineering
  • Verification signal: a deliberately faulty retry policy in staging triggers automatic rollback within the configured window; the us-east 2% pattern is documented as the standard rollout shape.
  • Rollback: rollback the gate policy only if it blocks a legitimate deploy — never by disabling the gate and the canary simultaneously.

P1-3 · Fix the rollback surface so a deploy revert also reverts flag-gated worker config

  • The 09:24 rollback consumed time without recovering service. Rollback procedures must enumerate every component touched by a release, including flag and worker config, and must not report "reverted" until a behavioural check passes.
  • Owner: Release engineering, with Queue service backend
  • Verification signal: a rollback drill in staging reverts both the binary and flag-gated config, and verification asserts non-zero backoff rather than a version string.
  • Rollback: n/a — this changes the rollback procedure itself.

P1-4 · Fix the runbook's first move for queue/backlog incidents

  • Remove "scale workers" as a default first action. Add a diagnostic gate: check retry rate and pool wait before scaling. Scaling is correct for a capacity-bound backlog and actively harmful against a shared-resource bottleneck, which is what happened at 09:14.
  • Owner: SRE on-call lead / incident management
  • Verification signal: runbook reviewed and signed off; the next DB-bound incident timeline shows a diagnostic step before any scaling decision.
  • Rollback: n/a — documentation.

P1-5 · Add the missing signals and alert on the causal metric, not the symptom

  • Instrument per-tenant retry attempt rate, resolved-policy value as a metric dimension, pool-wait histogram, connection-acquisition rate, and dead-letter counts. Alert on empty-policy resolution and on per-tenant retry rate.
  • Owner: Observability / SRE
  • Verification signal: a game day reproduces the empty-policy condition and the new alerts fire within 2 minutes — versus the observed 4-minute gap before a symptom alert, and a signal that was never alerted at all.
  • Rollback: disable or re-threshold individual alerts; avoid a big-bang dashboard change that removes existing symptom alerts before the new ones exist.

P2 — durability

P2-1 · Enforce a maximum per-region flag exposure ceiling

  • No region reaches 100% exposure on a retry or queue-affecting flag without an explicit, reviewed exception. Audit all regions' current percentages.
  • Owner: Platform
  • Verification signal: a config report shows no region above the ceiling without a recorded exception.
  • Rollback: n/a — reporting.

P2-2 · Add a failure-path integration test for retry behaviour

  • Simulate a failing job under the flag on and assert pacing, attempt cap, and disposition. Block release on this test.
  • Owner: Queue service backend
  • Verification signal: the test fails against 2026.09.23.1 and passes against the fixed build. (This is the regression test that would have caught the incident at 09:02.)
  • Rollback: n/a — test-only.

P2-3 · Review and ratify the postmortem

  • Walk INCIDENT.md with on-call, service, and database owners; capture corrections to the unknown list in Part 4 before the next release trains on it.
  • Owner: Incident commander
  • Verification signal: postmortem signed off by all three functions; the eight open unknowns are each marked resolved or explicitly accepted.
  • Rollback: n/a.

Part 3 — Sequencing and interlock

  • P0-1, P0-2, P0-3 must all land before the flag is re-enabled anywhere, even at the 2% canary level. P0-1 alone stops the storm; P0-2 alone bounds the blast radius if something else recurs; P0-3 alone prevents a recurrence at load time. Any one of them re-enabled alone leaves a known hole.
  • P0-4 runs in parallel and is not gated by the engineering fixes. It should not wait behind them — it is the item with direct customer consequences.
  • Re-enablement order: eu-west at 2% → hold through one peak → 25% → hold → 100%, with P1-5's alerts live and a named owner watching each step. Do not re-enable in us-east and eu-west simultaneously; the us-east cohort is what made the 2% pattern survivable the first time, and that evidence should not be spent twice in one step.
  • Do not bundle corrective actions. Several of these touch the same worker config path; bundling them makes the 09:24 rollback failure mode — an unverifiable state after a partial revert — more likely to repeat.

mcode-m3.1-flash/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Customer update — queued order processing, European region

Some customers in our European region saw slower processing of queued work, including longer checkout times, between approximately 09:07 and 10:18 UTC.

A software change released at 09:02 UTC affected how retry settings were resolved for individual accounts. For some accounts, no retry setting was found, and the system retried failed attempts immediately with no pause between them. That extra activity appears to have placed heavy demand on shared database connections, which slowed down work processing.

The effect was limited to our European region. Our US region, where the same change was active for only a small number of accounts, did not report this slowdown.

We rolled back part of the change at 09:24 UTC and fully disabled the affected setting at 09:43 UTC. Processing began recovering within two minutes, and the backlog was cleared by 10:18 UTC.

We are still reviewing whether any individual orders were delayed, repeated, or not completed. If we find any that were affected, we will contact those customers directly.

Body: 165 words (excluding this note and the heading).

mcode-m3.1-flash/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

INCIDENT.md — Queue processing degradation, eu-west

Analysis of TIMELINE.md and LOGS.txt only. No other data sources were consulted. Throughout, FACT marks something stated directly in the inputs and INFERENCE marks my interpretation of it. Hypotheses are stated with the evidence that would confirm or weaken them; no correlation below is treated as proof on its own.

1. Impact

Dimension Assessment Basis
Customer-visible duration ~71 minutes (09:07 → 10:18 UTC) FACT (timeline)
Window with confirmed customer symptoms ~09:11 → 10:18 (alerts on job age + checkout latency) FACT
Time to first alert after first anomaly 4 min (09:06:58 → 09:11) FACT
Geographic blast radius eu-west only; us-east used the same release with the flag on for 2% of tenants FACT
Tenant-level blast radius Not stated. Inferred to be "all tenants in eu-west" because the flag-off at 09:43 stopped the storm and eu-west saw full impact, vs 2% in us-east INFERENCE
Symptoms Growing queue depth, rising job age, elevated checkout latency, DB connection exhaustion FACT
Throughput Worker CPU fell 65% → 18% while queue depth rose FACT
Data loss / duplication / double-charge Unknown. No log line shows a job permanently failed, dead-lettered, or duplicated —
Financial / customer-count impact Unknown. No volume, tenant count, or order value in the inputs —

Honest scoping of impact: the inputs let me state duration, region, and symptom type with confidence. They do not let me state how many customers or orders were affected, or whether any job was lost. retry_budget_exhausted (09:06:58, 09:35:12) means the retry budget was consumed; the inputs contain no evidence of what the system did with the job afterwards (dead-letter? drop? hold in queue?). That is the single largest customer-facing unknown and it drives the first corrective action in ACTIONS.md.

2. Concise timeline (UTC)

Time Event Type Source
09:02 Deploy api 2026.09.23.1; change adds tenant-specific retry policy FACT timeline
09:06:58 worker-17: job 81 (tenant acme) attempt 1, 2 and 3 all result=retry, all delay_ms=0, within the same second. retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=41 FACT logs
09:07 Queue depth begins rising in eu-west; worker CPU falls 65% → 18% FACT timeline
09:07:01 worker-22: db pool wait exceeded wait_ms=5000 active=10 idle=0 FACT logs
09:07:03 worker-08: job 92 (tenant beta) succeeds, elapsed_ms=830 FACT logs
09:11 Alerts fire for job age and checkout latency FACT timeline
09:14 On-call increases workers 40 → 90; queue depth rises faster FACT timeline
09:19:52 api-03: db remaining connection slots reserved FACT logs
09:20 DB connections reach configured maximum of 300 FACT timeline
09:24 API deploy rolled back; new job creation normalizes, backlog still stuck FACT timeline
09:31:10 A worker pool is restarted; worker-51 starts with config_retry_default="exponential" feature_flag=true FACT logs
~09:35:12 worker-51 (4 min after restart): job 319 retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=38 FACT logs
09:43:07 worker-51: config_changed feature_flag=false FACT logs
09:43:09 job 319 attempt 1 result=retry — now with delay_ms=1000 FACT logs
09:43:10 job 319 attempt 2 result=success elapsed_ms=190 FACT logs
09:43 Flag tenant_retry_policy disabled; recovery begins within 2 minutes FACT timeline
10:18 Backlog cleared FACT timeline
— us-east: same release, flag on for 2% of tenants, no alert fired FACT timeline

Two ordering details carry most of the analytical weight:

  1. 09:06:58 precedes 09:07. The first logged failure is before the timeline's first symptom. The retry storm started first; the queue merely grew afterwards.
  2. 09:24 rollback did not clear the backlog while 09:43 flag-off did. These are two attempts to remove the change, one of which failed. That asymmetry is the most informative single fact in the dataset.

3. Most likely root cause

Confidence: high for the causal chain, low for the specific defect.

A defect in the tenant-specific retry-policy path introduced by 2026.09.23.1 resolved to an empty policy (policy="") for the affected tenants. An empty policy produced zero backoff (delay_ms=0), so every failed attempt was retried immediately, in a tight loop. Those retries consumed shared database connections faster than the pool could release them, starving workers of connections (pool wait exceeded wait_ms=5000), which throttled job throughput and let the queue grow. Disabling the feature flag at 09:43 restored the working exponential default backoff, retries became paced, and the queue drained.

The chain, step by step — each link and its support:

  1. Tenant retry policy resolves to an empty string. FACT that policy="" is logged at 09:06:58 and 09:35:12. INFERENCE that this is the new code path misbehaving, because policy="" appears only in the window where the flag is on, and disappears after the flag is off.
  2. Empty policy means no backoff. FACT that all three attempts of job 81 carry delay_ms=0 at 09:06:58, and that the whole budget burned in elapsed_ms=41. INFERENCE that delay_ms is sourced from the resolved policy, so an empty policy yields zero. The 1000 ms delay at 09:43:09 (same worker, same job, flag off) is the positive control.
  3. Immediate retries generate connection pressure. INFERENCE, and it is the weakest link in the chain. It assumes each attempt acquires a DB connection and holds it through the failure path. The inputs do not show connection lifecycle. What the inputs do show is the downstream consequence — pool waits and the 300-connection cap — and CPU falling to 18%, which is the signature of threads blocked on I/O rather than spinning. This is a plausible mechanism, not a proven one.
  4. Connection starvation throttled throughput. FACT (pool waits, reserved-slot error, cap reached, queue growth, CPU drop). This link is strong.
  5. Removing the flag removed the storm. FACT, with a near-controlled comparison: same worker (51), same job (319), four minutes apart, behaviour flips from retry_budget_exhausted policy="" to delay_ms=1000 → success, two seconds after config_changed feature_flag=false.

What the root cause is not: not the API binary alone (its 09:24 rollback failed to recover the backlog while a flag flip succeeded), and not corrupted worker state (the 09:31 restart worked cleanly for four minutes, then reproduced the same policy="" failure). The defect lives in the flag-gated retry configuration path, which the API rollback did not revert.

4. Contributing factors

Ordered by how much they amplified impact. These are separate from the root cause: fixing the retry-policy defect would end the incident, but these are what made it large, slow to detect, and slow to fix.

  1. No backoff on unresolved configuration (FACT). delay_ms=0 for every attempt. A configuration that resolves to "nothing" degraded into the most aggressive retry behaviour instead of falling back to the safe default that was demonstrably present (config_retry_default="exponential", logged at 09:31:10). Failing open into a thundering herd is the core design failure.
  2. Retries continued past retry_budget_exhausted (FACT, INFERENCE on meaning). A retry budget was reached in 41 ms and the job kept cycling. A budget that does not stop the retry loop provides no protection.
  3. Unbounded blast radius from flag configuration (FACT). 100% exposure in eu-west vs 2% in us-east on the identical binary. The severity of this incident was decided by configuration, not by code quality — the same release was survivable in us-east only because few tenants had the flag on. "No alert fired in us-east" is not evidence of "no impact in us-east": at 2% exposure the signal may simply have been below the alert threshold.
  4. Scaling workers against a shared bottleneck (FACT + INFERENCE). 40 → 90 workers at 09:14 while the bottleneck was a shared, capped resource. The queue rose faster afterwards. INFERENCE: because more workers were blocked on the same constrained pool, the extra workers added connection demand without adding service capacity. Note the confounder: queue depth is (arrival rate − service rate), so faster growth could also reflect rising arrival rate rather than the scaling itself. Moderate confidence that this amplified the incident; it did not cause it, since degradation began 7 minutes earlier.
  5. Incomplete rollback surface (FACT). The API rollback at 09:24 left the system in the incident state for another 19 minutes. A deploy rollback that does not revert flag-gated worker configuration gives a misleading "we have reverted" signal. The team lost ~19 minutes on a mitigation that could not work.
  6. Restart as an ineffective mitigation (FACT). The 09:31 restart produced 4 good minutes and then reproduced the defect. It also created a false signal that the problem was transient. Useful as a falsification of the corrupted-worker-state hypothesis; wasted as a mitigation.
  7. Detection was symptom-based (FACT). The first observable was queue depth at 09:07; the first alert at 09:11. The directly causal signal — a resolved policy that is empty, and retry rate per tenant — was never reported, at any point, by any system or person. It is present in the log data the whole time.
  8. An unexplained 5-minute latency between deploy and first failure (FACT about the gap). 09:02 → 09:06:58. INFERENCE: the trigger is a recurring workload pattern rather than a deploy-time event, since a freshly booted worker also took ~4 minutes to reproduce (09:31:10 → 09:35:12). Unresolved: what the trigger condition actually is.

5. Hypotheses, with evidence for and against

H1 — Retry storm from unresolved tenant retry policy exhausted DB connections (accepted, high confidence in the chain)

Supporting

  • policy="" at 09:06:58 and 09:35:12 — the signal is present in the data twice.
  • delay_ms=0 on all three attempts of job 81 within one second, budget exhausted in 41 ms.
  • Flag off at 09:43 → delay_ms=1000 on the same job and worker within 2 s → success. This is the closest thing to a controlled experiment in the dataset.
  • CPU dropped 65% → 18%: workers were blocked, not spinning.
  • 09:24 API rollback failed to recover; 09:43 flag-off did. The change that mattered was flag-gated, not binary-gated.
  • A fresh worker (09:31) reproduced the failure with feature_flag=true and a correct config_retry_default — so the default was healthy and the tenant-specific override path was not.

Weakening / limits

  • No connection-lifecycle data. The link "zero-delay retries consume connections" is inferred from downstream symptoms, not observed.
  • The delay_ms → resolved-policy dependency is inferred from one before/after pair (job 319), not a documented config schema.
  • Job 319 still needed two attempts after the flag was off. Backoff fixed the pacing, not an underlying per-job error. Some real failure condition remains unexplained; only the amplification was removed.
  • Single-tenant evidence (acme failed at 09:06:58; beta succeeded at 09:07:03). Tenant attribution is thin.

H2 — Pure database capacity shortfall (rejected as root cause; contributory)

Weakening

  • The 300-connection cap was not reached until 09:20, but degradation started at 09:07 — 13 minutes earlier.
  • worker-22 at 09:07:01 reports pool wait exceeded with only active=10 idle=0, i.e. saturation while total usage was nowhere near 300. The binding constraint was already worker-local or hold-time driven, not global capacity.
  • A pure capacity problem would not be resolved by disabling a retry-policy flag within 2 minutes.
  • A pure capacity problem does not explain policy="".

Retained as contributory: low connection headroom plus worker scaling at 09:14 is why the situation escalated to a hard, system-wide failure instead of a slow degradation. Capacity was the victim, not the culprit.

H3 — Worker scaling (40 → 90) as root cause (rejected as root cause; contributory)

Weakening

  • Onset at 09:07 precedes the 09:14 scale-up by 7 minutes.
  • The flag-off at 09:43 restored throughput without reducing worker count.
  • policy="" is unexplained by scaling.

Retained as contributory: see CF4 above, with the arrival-rate confounder stated.

H4 — Poison jobs / corrupt stuck backlog (mostly rejected, not fully excluded)

Weakening

  • A restarted pool processed jobs normally for four minutes — inconsistent with a permanently wedged head-of-line job.
  • The 09:24 rollback left the backlog stuck while new creation normalized, which fits workers continuing to churn existing jobs under the old flag, not a data defect.
  • Recovery at 09:43 was immediate and complete, not gradual. A corrupt job would typically be skipped, not heal.
  • The 09:43 success took one retried attempt, so the failure is not fully explained away.

Not excluded because: the inputs never show the final disposition of job 81, and no log line shows a dead-letter or drop path. A subset of jobs could have been individually poisoned while the bulk incident was H1. This is why the job-outcome audit in ACTIONS.md is P1 rather than P2.

H5 — Bad configuration value versus code defect in policy resolution (unresolved; both inside H1)

Supporting code-defect reading: config_retry_default="exponential" was present and correct at 09:31:10 (FACT) yet policy="" was still produced (FACT). A bad default therefore does not explain it; the tenant-specific lookup or the flag-gated code path did not resolve. Supporting config reading: a missing or malformed entry in the per-tenant policy map would produce an empty string without any code error, and would naturally be tenant-scoped — which would also explain why beta (09:07:03) behaved differently from acme. Status: the two readings predict the same evidence so far. I cannot distinguish them from these inputs, and I am explicitly not claiming the code is at fault rather than the config. This is why data request #1 in RESPONSE.md targets the resolver diff plus the live policy map.

H6 — Runaway CPU loop in the new retry path (rejected)

Weakening: worker CPU fell to 18%. A hot loop would raise CPU. The observed profile is blocking, not spinning.

6. What remains unknown

Ordered by how much it changes the conclusions above.

  1. What the policy resolver actually did. Why did it return "" — missing map entry, tenant-key mismatch, parse failure, or an error swallowed into an empty string? Determines whether the fix is code, config, or both. (H5, data request #1.)
  2. The connection lifecycle under retry. Are connections held through the failure path, leaked, or simply requested at excessive rate? Determines whether the fix must include connection budgeting, not just backoff. (H1 link 3, data request #2.)
  3. Job dispositions, 09:07–10:18. How many jobs failed permanently, were dead-lettered, dropped, duplicated, or double-executed? This is the customer-facing unknown with the largest consequences and it is completely absent from the inputs.
  4. The true exposure and customer impact. Tenant and order counts, affected checkout volume, whether us-east's 2% cohort had any impact that stayed below the alert threshold, and SLO/error-budget consumption.
  5. Why the API rollback did not revert the behaviour. Was the flag independently managed, or did the rollback path omit worker config? Without this, future rollbacks carry the same 19-minute trap.
  6. The 5-minute trigger delay (09:02 → 09:06:58, and 09:31:10 → 09:35:12). What workload condition provokes the first failure? Relevant to how quickly a fix can be validated.
  7. Why policy="" and retry_budget_exhausted never alerted. Were these metrics unmonitored, monitored without thresholds, or simply invisible? Distinguishes "no telemetry" from "telemetry nobody read".
  8. Whether workers were the first or second victim. 09:07:01 shows worker-22 pool-waiting 13 minutes before the API hit reserved-slot errors at 09:19:52 — workers failed first, but the inputs do not show why the API was spared for so long.

qwen3.8-max/09-incident-analysis/RESPONSE.md

Response — confidence and next data requests

Confidence level

Overall: high confidence in the trigger, medium in the exact mechanism, low on blast-radius details.

  • High (~90%): the tenant_retry_policy flag-gated tenant retry policy was the trigger. Direct log evidence: policy="" with delay_ms=0 and retry budgets exhausted in ~40 ms; the same failing job (319) retried at delay_ms=1000 and succeeded within seconds of flag-off; recovery began within 2 minutes of the flag change; the restarted pool came up with the flag still true and stalled again with the same signature; the API rollback alone did not clear the backlog. The US contrast (same release, 2% flag scope, no alerts) corroborates but is correlation, not proof.
  • Medium (~70–75%): zero-delay retry churn → DB connection saturation (cap 300) → worker starvation is the stall mechanism. Timing and pool-wait logs (wait_ms=5000, CPU 65%→18%, API "slots reserved") are consistent, and worker scale-out (40→90) worsening the queue fits contention — but there are no query-level DB metrics attributing connections to retry loops, and the worker scale-out effect is confounded with the underlying storm.
  • Medium (~65%): the flag persisted because rollback does not cover flag state. Strongly suggested by the timeline (rollback 09:24; feature_flag=true at 09:31:10; exhaustion at 09:35:12), but whether the deploy also wrote persistent policy data that the rollback left behind is unverified.
  • Low/unknown: why the policy resolved to an empty string; which tenants were affected (sample: acme failing, beta healthy); the fate of retry-budget-exhausted jobs (possible silent loss — job 319's later success hints at requeue, job 81 is unaccounted for); total customer impact.

I treated the deploy→onset and US-region contrasts as supporting correlation only; the load-bearing evidence is the flag-off → recovery sequence with matching log signatures on both sides of the change.

Next two pieces of data I would request

  1. Tenant retry-policy configuration and resolution path for the incident window: the config store contents (per-tenant policies for eu-west, e.g., acme vs beta), the flag targeting rules (what % of eu-west tenants were enabled), and the worker/API code path that resolves the policy — to determine why lookups returned "" (missing entries, lookup bug, cache, or data written by the deploy that rollback did not undo). This closes the root-cause mechanism.
  2. Job disposition and completion reconciliation for all retry_budget_exhausted jobs between 09:07 and 09:43 (plus DLQ/requeue records and per-tenant checkout success/failure/latency metrics): to determine whether any work was silently dropped or duplicated and to quantify real customer impact. This closes the largest open risk.

(If a third were allowed: DB server query/connection-level metrics for 09:05–09:45, to directly confirm the saturation mechanism.)

Actions — eu-west queue stall incident

Owners are roles, not individuals. Priorities: containment first, then P1 (this week), then P2 (1–2 weeks).

Immediate containment (0–24 h)

C1. Verify and freeze tenant_retry_policy OFF in all regions/environments

  • Action: Confirm flag state is 0% everywhere (eu-west, US, staging); place a change freeze on re-enabling it until fixes A1–A2 ship and are verified.
  • Owner: On-call SRE, with the queue-service owner.
  • Verification: Flag console shows 0% globally; zero new retry_budget_exhausted policy="" log lines; queue depth, job age, and checkout latency at baseline for 24 h.
  • Rollback considerations: This is the rollback of the feature. Any future re-enable must go through a staged canary (A5) — never a broad flip — and must have a documented one-command kill switch.

C2. Reconcile and reprocess jobs that exhausted their retry budget (09:07–09:43)

  • Action: Count all retry_budget_exhausted events in the window (e.g., jobs 81, 319); match against completed/DLQ/requeued records; identify any dropped or duplicated work; reprocess what is missing.
  • Owner: Queue-service engineering.
  • Verification: Reconciliation report with zero unaccounted jobs; reprocessing completes with ~100% success; duplicate-side-effect checks pass (no double charges/shipments).
  • Rollback considerations: Bulk replay must be idempotent and deduplicated; be ready to pause replay immediately on signs of double processing.

C3. Quantify customer impact and notify affected tenants

  • Action: Pull eu-west checkout success/failure/latency per tenant for 09:07–10:18; build the affected-tenant list; send the customer update (CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md) and follow up individually where jobs failed or were delayed.
  • Owner: Incident commander with the support/comms lead.
  • Verification: Impact numbers reconciled against alerting/metrics; notifications sent to all affected tenants; support ticket volume for the window triaged.
  • Rollback considerations: None technical; if impact numbers change after deeper audit (C2), issue a prompt correction rather than leaving the first estimate standing.

C4. Elevated monitoring for 48 h

  • Action: Watch DB pool utilization, queue depth/age, worker CPU, retry rate and delay_ms distribution; add a temporary alert on any policy="" warning and on DB pool > 80% of cap.
  • Owner: On-call SRE.
  • Verification: Dashboards and thresholds live; no recurrence alerts in 48 h.
  • Rollback considerations: None; remove temporary alerts after the review to avoid noise.

Corrective actions — P1 (this week)

A1. Safe default when tenant retry policy resolution is empty/invalid

  • Action: In the policy resolution path, fall back to config_retry_default (exponential backoff with jitter) whenever a tenant policy is missing/empty/invalid; enforce a minimum retry delay (never 0 ms); emit a warning log + metric on every fallback.
  • Owner: Worker/API service development lead.
  • Verification: Unit/integration tests cover missing, empty, and malformed policies; a staging drill with a deliberately empty policy shows backoff behavior and no pool stress; policy="" warnings impossible in prod without an accompanying fallback metric.
  • Rollback considerations: Ship as a normal deploy with a quick rollback path; behavior for valid policies must be unchanged — verify via tests/shadow traffic before rollout.

A2. Retry-storm guardrails

  • Action: Rate-limit retries per job and per tenant; add a circuit breaker on the retry_budget_exhausted rate (stop-and-alert instead of churning); cap requeue frequency for exhausted jobs.
  • Owner: Queue-service engineering lead.
  • Verification: Fault-injection load test (simulate empty policy): DB pool stays < 80%, queue keeps draining healthy tenants, breaker alert fires within ~1 minute.
  • Rollback considerations: Guardrails behind their own flag; if a guardrail misfires and suppresses legitimate retries, disable that guardrail first — ensure defaults are fail-safe (fall back to prior behavior, not to zero delay).

A3. Couple feature-flag state to release rollback

  • Action: Tooling + runbook change: rolling back a release automatically disables (or explicitly prompts about) flags introduced or broadened by that release; incident rollback checklists must include flag kill switches.
  • Owner: Release engineering lead.
  • Verification: Rollback drill shows flag state reverted/prompted; updated runbook reviewed in the postmortem; measured diagnosis-to-recovery time improves in the next drill.
  • Rollback considerations: Provide a logged override for cases where the flag is genuinely unrelated to the defect, so auto-revert cannot itself cause a second incident.

A4. DB connection governance

  • Action: Per-component connection budgets (worker pools vs reserved API slots) with admission control; alert at 80% of the 300 cap; bound worker autoscaling by available connection headroom; reassess whether 300 matches the DB server's real capacity.
  • Owner: Platform database lead with SRE.
  • Verification: Staging drill: worker spike no longer starves the API; alert fires before the cap; documented max worker count × pool size ≤ cap with margin.
  • Rollback considerations: Raising the cap risks shifting saturation to the DB server — stage any change and watch DB CPU/latency; be prepared to revert the cap quickly.

Corrective actions — P2 (1–2 weeks)

A5. Consistent staged flag rollouts in every region

  • Action: Enforce ramp policies (e.g., 2% → 10% → 50% → 100% with dwell time and alert-gated auto-rollback) for behavior-changing flags in all regions; review why eu-west got broad enablement while US sat at 2%.
  • Owner: Feature-flag platform owner with the service owner.
  • Verification: Flag tooling rejects region-skipping ramps for protected flags; eu-west rollout history reviewed and findings in the postmortem.
  • Rollback considerations: Auto-rollback thresholds must be tuned/tested to avoid flapping; keep a manual override with audit logging.

A6. Scaling runbook fix

  • Action: Document the 09:14 lesson: do not scale workers out while DB pool waits are elevated or connections are near cap; check connection headroom first; prefer fixing throughput blockers over adding consumers.
  • Owner: SRE lead.
  • Verification: Runbook published; walked through in a game-day/tabletop exercise.
  • Rollback considerations: None.

A7. Observability for effective retry behavior

  • Action: Sampled per-job logging of the effective policy (tenant, policy id, delay), dashboards for retry rate / delay_ms distribution / budget-exhaustion rate, alert on delay_ms=0 patterns.
  • Owner: Service owning team.
  • Verification: Staging drill: an injected zero-delay retry pattern is visible on the dashboard and alerts within ~1 minute.
  • Rollback considerations: Keep logging sampled to avoid cost/performance regression; sampling rate adjustable without deploy.

A8. Blameless postmortem and action tracking

  • Rollback considerations: None.

qwen3.8-max/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Customer update: eu-west checkout delays

All times UTC. Checkout processing in our Europe (eu-west) region was delayed from about 09:07 until 10:18, when the outstanding backlog fully cleared. Many customers in the region likely saw slower checkouts during that window; we are still confirming whether any transactions failed.

A recent release introduced a new per-customer retry setting. The evidence so far indicates that for some customers this setting resolved to an empty policy, so the system retried work instantly instead of pausing between attempts. That exhausted a shared database connection limit and stalled the queue. Rolling back the release alone did not clear the backlog; switching the new setting off at 09:43 restored normal processing within minutes. Our US region ran the same release with the setting enabled for only a small share of customers and saw no impact.

The setting remains off while we add safeguards, verify that no jobs were lost, and complete a full review. We will publish a detailed report and contact any customers whose transactions were affected. We're sorry for the disruption.

qwen3.8-max/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident analysis — eu-west queue stall and checkout latency

Sources: TIMELINE.md, LOGS.txt (only evidence available). Date: not stated in sources; release identifier 2026.09.23.1 suggests 2026-09-23 [inferred]. Convention: [observed] = stated directly in the sources; [inferred] = interpretation, may be revised with more data.

1. Impact

  • [observed] eu-west job processing degraded from ~09:07; recovery began ~09:45 (within two minutes of the 09:43 flag change); backlog fully cleared 10:18. Degradation ≈ 38 minutes; backlog residence up to ≈ 71 minutes.
  • [observed] Alerts fired 09:11 for job age and checkout latency → customer-facing checkout delays in eu-west. [inferred] Customers in eu-west experienced slow or delayed checkouts for most of that window.
  • [observed] API hosts hit "db remaining connection slots reserved" errors (09:19:52); new job creation only normalized after the 09:24 rollback → job intake was impaired for part of the window.
  • [observed] DB connections reached the configured maximum (300) at 09:20; worker CPU fell 65% → 18% (workers blocked/idle, not busy).
  • [observed] US region: same release, flag enabled for only 2% of tenants, no alerts. [inferred] Minimal or no US customer impact.
  • Unknown: whether any jobs that hit retry_budget_exhausted between 09:07 and 09:43 were permanently dropped (silent work loss) vs. requeued; the full set of affected tenants; quantified checkout failure/abandonment counts. Job 319 exhausted at 09:35 and later succeeded at 09:43 [observed], which suggests exhausted jobs were not necessarily lost — but disposition of all such jobs (e.g., job 81) is unverified.

2. Concise timeline (UTC)

Time Event Source
09:02 API deploy 2026.09.23.1; adds tenant-specific retry policy timeline [observed]
09:06:58 worker-17: job 81 (tenant acme) retries 3× in the same second, all delay_ms=0; retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=41 logs [observed]
09:07 eu-west queue depth begins rising; worker CPU falls 65% → 18% timeline [observed]
09:07:01 worker-22: DB pool wait exceeded 5000 ms, active=10 idle=0 logs [observed]
09:07:03 job 92 (tenant beta) succeeds on attempt 1 (830 ms) — not all tenants affected logs [observed]
09:11 Alerts: job age, checkout latency timeline [observed]
09:14 On-call scales workers 40 → 90; queue depth rises faster timeline [observed]
09:19:52 api-03: "db remaining connection slots reserved" logs [observed]
09:20 DB connections reach configured max (300) timeline [observed]
09:24 API deploy rolled back; new job creation normalizes; backlog still stuck timeline [observed]
09:31 One worker pool restarted; worker-51 starts with config_retry_default="exponential" feature_flag=true; pool processes normally ~4 min timeline+logs [observed]
09:35:12 worker-51: job 319 retry_budget_exhausted policy="" elapsed_ms=38; pool stalls (~4 min after 09:31:10 startup — timing matches) timeline+logs [observed]
09:43:07 Flag tenant_retry_policy disabled (config_changed feature_flag=false) logs [observed]

context US: same release, flag at 2% of tenants, no alerts timeline [observed]

3. Most likely root cause

[inferred, high confidence in trigger; medium confidence in full mechanism]

The flag-gated tenant-specific retry policy (tenant_retry_policy) resolved to an empty policy (policy="") for affected tenants, causing workers to retry with zero delay (delay_ms=0 observed), exhausting jobs' retry budgets in tens of milliseconds (41 ms, 38 ms observed) and cycling failing work at a very high rate. This retry storm saturated the shared DB connection pool (max 300 reached; pool waits > 5 s; API slots reserved), starving workers (CPU 65% → 18% — blocked, not busy) and stalling the eu-west queue. Disabling the flag at 09:43 restored the default exponential backoff (delay_ms=1000 observed, consistent with config_retry_default="exponential" logged at startup) and processing recovered within two minutes.

Why the flag, not the deploy code itself, is the operative trigger [inferred]:

  1. Symptoms persisted 19 minutes after the API rollback (09:24) and cleared only after flag-off (09:43) [observed timing].
  2. The restarted worker pool came up with feature_flag=true (rollback did not change flag state) and stalled again after ~4 minutes, with the same policy="" exhaustion signature [observed].
  3. US ran the same release with the flag at 2% of tenants and had no alerts — a natural control group [observed; correlation, strong but not proof].
  4. After flag-off, the previously failing job 319 immediately retried with a 1000 ms delay and succeeded [observed].

Why the policy resolved to "" is unknown (missing tenant config, lookup bug, or bad data written by the deploy — see §6).

4. Contributing factors

  1. [observed action / inferred effect] Worker scale-out 40 → 90 at 09:14 increased contention for the capped DB connections; queue depth rose faster afterward. With per-worker pools of ~10 connections (active=10 observed), 40+ workers can exceed the global 300 cap on demand alone — scaling made saturation worse, not better.
  2. [observed gap] No safe fallback when the tenant policy resolved empty: a default existed (config_retry_default="exponential") yet the effective policy was "" with delay_ms=0. No validation or alert on policy="".
  3. [observed] Deploy rollback did not disable the feature flag; flag lifecycle is decoupled from release rollback, and the restart runbook brought workers back up with the flag still on. Diagnosis cost ~19 extra minutes (09:24 → 09:43).
  4. [observed] A single shared DB connection cap (300) for workers and API with no isolation/admission control; the API was starved ("slots reserved") by worker-side contention.
  5. [inferred] Retry-budget-exhausted jobs appear to re-enter the queue (job 319 reprocessed at 09:43), amplifying churn while the misbehavior persisted. Exact disposition path unknown.
  6. [observed contrast / inferred process gap] Flag rollout scoping was inconsistent: broad in eu-west vs 2% in US. A staged canary in eu-west would have limited blast radius.

5. Hypotheses and evidence

H1 — Flag-gated tenant retry policy resolved empty → zero-delay retry storm → DB pool saturation (leading hypothesis)

  • Supports: policy="" with delay_ms=0 and budget exhaustion in ~40 ms [observed]; flag-off → delay_ms=1000 and immediate success of the same failing job [observed]; restarted pool with flag=true stalled again with the same signature [observed]; recovery began within 2 min of flag-off [observed]; US control (same release, 2% flag scope, no alerts) [observed]; healthy tenant beta at 09:07:03 is consistent with tenant-scoped misbehavior [observed].
  • Weakens: no query-level DB metrics directly attributing connection consumption to retry loops [absent data]; the reason for the empty resolution is not logged [absent data]; visible sample is tiny (2 tenants, 2 failing jobs) — affected-tenant scope unverified.

H2 — Non-flag code defect in release 2026.09.23.1

  • Supports: onset ~5 min after deploy [observed]; API rollback normalized new job creation [observed].
  • Weakens: backlog symptoms persisted 19 min after rollback and ended only at flag-off [observed]; restarted pool with the rolled-back API still stalled [observed]; US unaffected on the same release [observed]. The deploy may still have written persistent config/data that made policies resolve empty — unverified.

H3 — DB capacity/connection-limit problem as primary cause

  • Supports: pool waits > 5 s, cap of 300 reached, API slot-reservation errors [observed].
  • Weakens: saturation began after the retry misbehavior (09:06:58–09:07:01) [observed ordering]; worker CPU fell (workers blocked, not overloaded with legitimate work) [observed]; recovery occurred with no DB change, purely flag-off [observed]. DB saturation is best explained as downstream of H1.

H4 — Worker scale-out (40 → 90) caused the stall

  • Supports: queue depth rose faster after 09:14; cap hit at 09:20 [observed].
  • Weakens: degradation began 09:07, before the scale-out [observed ordering]; scale-out was a response, not a trigger; recovery followed flag-off with no reported scale-down [observed]. It is an accelerator/contributor, not the cause.

H5 — eu-west regional infrastructure fault (DB instance, network)

  • Supports: only eu-west alerted [observed].
  • Weakens: the regions differ in flag scope (2% in US) [observed], which explains the difference without infrastructure; no infra errors in logs [absent data]; an application config change (flag-off) produced recovery within 2 minutes [observed].

Note on correlation: the deploy→onset and US-contrast observations are correlations; the strongest causal evidence is the flag-off → recovery sequence with matching log signatures before and after.

6. What remains unknown

  1. Why policy resolution returned "" for affected tenants: missing tenant entries, lookup/validation bug, stale cache, or bad data written by the deploy that the rollback did not undo. Needs the config store contents and resolution code path.
  2. Which tenants were affected: flag targeting in eu-west (assume broad vs US 2%, but unverified); acme failing and beta healthy is a sample of two.
  3. Fate of retry-budget-exhausted jobs during 09:07–09:43 (dropped, DLQ, requeued): possible silent work loss or duplicated side effects; job 319's later success hints at requeue, but job 81 and the full population are unaccounted for.
  4. Quantified customer impact: checkout latency distributions, failed/abandoned checkouts, per-tenant effects, revenue impact.
  5. Flag governance: why the rollback did not disable the flag (process gap vs. tooling limitation), and why eu-west enablement scope differed from US.
  6. Restarted-pool mechanics: why the 09:31 restart processed normally for exactly ~4 minutes (fresh connections? cache warm-up? job mix?) before stalling.
  7. Whether the flag was ever also serving a needed function — i.e., cost of leaving it off (assumed low; US ran at 2% without alerts, but unverified).
  8. Exact incident date (release name suggests 2026-09-23; not stated).

Response — confidence, reasoning, and next data requests

Confidence in the analysis

Overall confidence in the root cause: moderate to high (~75%).

Breakdown:

Claim Confidence Basis
The immediate failure domain was database connection saturation starving job processing High (~90%) Pool wait of 5000 ms with active=10 idle=0 at 09:07:01, connections at the configured maximum of 300 at 09:20, remaining connection slots reserved, and worker CPU falling 65% → 18% (blocking on I/O, not CPU saturation)
The controlling variable was the tenant_retry_policy flag, not the deployed binary High (~90%) The API deploy was rolled back at 09:24 and the backlog stayed stuck; disabling the flag at 09:43 was followed by recovery within two minutes and by retries switching from delay_ms=0 to delay_ms=1000. This is a within-incident controlled comparison
The defect is zero backoff when the tenant policy resolves to an empty value, instead of a fallback to the default exponential policy Moderate (~75%) policy="" alongside delay_ms=0 at 09:06:58 and again at 09:35:12 after a restart that loaded a healthy config_retry_default="exponential", which rules out the default config as the empty source
The precise code or configuration defect (which lookup failed, and why) Low (~30%) No config-store history, policy-resolution logs, or code diff for 2026.09.23.1 in the provided data
User/business impact scope, and whether jobs ran more than once Low (~20%) No job counts, tenant list, traffic metric, or side-effect ledger in the provided data

Where I could be wrong. The evidence is consistent with H1 (zero-delay retry storm), but consistency is not proof — the retry behaviour, the flag disable, and the DB saturation all moved in a sequence that fits more than one story. Specifically, a pre-existing DB connection leak or a slow-query regression could have been the primary fault, with the retry behaviour as an aggravating amplifier rather than the trigger. The strongest argument against that is that recovery followed a config change with no capacity change, but a leak plus normal load recovery over ~35 minutes cannot be excluded without pool metrics. The US region's quiet behaviour is supportive but not decisive: the exposure was only ~2% of tenants, which may simply have kept the effect below alert thresholds.

I have deliberately kept the DB pool sizing question separate: hitting 300 connections shows a ceiling was reached, not that 300 was needed, so the provided data does not justify a specific new capacity number.

Next two pieces of data I would request

1. The tenant retry policy configuration as it resolved at 09:06 in eu-west, plus the diff of 2026.09.23.1's retry code path. Specifically: what the flag tenant_retry_policy contained per tenant at 09:00–09:07 UTC; whether eu-west tenants had a policy entry at all; the config-store write history and the merge/override order between the default exponential config and the tenant override; and the code diff showing where the empty string is consumed. This is the single highest-value item: it converts the leading hypothesis from "fits the evidence" into either a confirmed defect or a refuted one, and it directly determines whether the fix is a fallback bug, a config rollout gap, or something else entirely.

2. A job-level execution ledger and database connection metrics for 09:00–10:30 UTC. Specifically, per job: tenant, attempt count, timestamps, outcomes and side-effect markers — to quantify duplicate executions and the true count of affected jobs, tenants, and checkout orders. Alongside it, DB connection pool metrics over the window: active/idle counts, wait-time distribution, and per-connection hold time versus query time, to test for a connection leak or slow queries independent of retry volume, and to process the two traffic-surge questions: request volume before and during the incident, to rule out or in an organic workload spike.

The first request targets causation; the second targets impact and an alternative hypothesis. Together they would either confirm the analysis at high confidence or redirect it.

Corrective and containment actions

Roles used as owners: Incident lead (on-call IC), Service owner (API), Service owner (worker runtime), Release engineering, SRE / observability, Database owner, Support / customer ops.

Time to implement and verification signals assume the system is currently in a recovered state (backlog cleared 10:18 UTC). Every action should be re-validated against current state before execution.

A. Immediate containment (now)

# Action Owner Verification signal Rollback / risk
A1 Keep tenant_retry_policy disabled globally and confirm the flag state from an independent source (config store / API), not just from a worker's self-reported state. Incident lead Flag endpoint reports off in all regions; no new retry_budget_exhausted or delay_ms=0 entries for 30 min Disabling the flag disables the feature entirely; intended behaviour for the tenant-specific policy is off until A2/P1 lands
A2 Freeze api 2026.09.23.1 or any related rollout — no re-deploy, no config push touching the retry path — until the empty-policy defect is understood. Release engineering No builds promoted for the retry path; release dashboard shows freeze None (blocks delivery); revert by lifting the freeze once P1-1 is merged

Service owner (worker runtime) + Support / customer ops Reconciled count of duplicate executions; explicit "no impact" or a list of affected records None (read-only)

B. Prioritized corrective actions

P1 — Fix the defect (highest priority)

P1-1. Make an empty or unreadable tenant policy fall back to the default exponential policy, and add a validation layer that treats delay_ms=0 on a user-facing retry as invalid.

  • Owner: Service owner (worker runtime), with Service owner (API) for the policy source.
  • Verification signals: unit tests (empty policy → default exponential, missing policy → default exponential, malformed policy → logged + default); a synthetic job for a tenant with no policy configured shows delay_ms >= base; delay_ms=0 occurrences = 0 in production; a new policy_fallback_applied counter/meter is visible and tracked.
  • Rollback: guard the whole change behind the existing tenant_retry_policy flag; if the fix misbehaves, the kill switch is a flag disable (proven at 09:43).
  • Evidence basis: policy="" with delay_ms=0 at 09:06:58 and 09:35:12, versus delay_ms=1000 after the flag was disabled.

P1-2. Alert on the real failure signal, not the downstream symptom.

  • Owner: SRE / observability.
  • Verification signals: alert fires within 60 s on a synthetic delay_ms=0 or empty-policy event; alert fires on a sustained retry_budget_exhausted rate per worker; alerts confirmed routed to on-call.
  • Verification that it would have caught this incident: replay the 09:06:58 log line against the rule and confirm a page.
  • Rollback: alerts only — no runtime behaviour changes.

P1-3. Stage flag rollouts instead of switching all tenants at once.

  • Owner: Release engineering.
  • Verification signals: rollout limited to a small tenant percentage with automatic comparison of queue depth, job age and retry rate against control before promotion; the rollout halts on breach; the flag kill switch is exercised in a game-day.
  • Rollback: kill switch per cohort; the 2% US exposure is the evidence that limited exposure contained impact.
  • Note: the exact percentage that would have kept eu-west quiet is unknown; start conservative and expand on evidence, not on a schedule.

P2 — Make the failure mode survivable

P2-1. Add a retry-storm brake: per-tenant and global retry-rate caps, jitter/bounded backoff, and concurrency limits for retries versus first attempts.

  • Owner: Service owner (worker runtime).
  • Verification signals: load test showing sustained retry volume while DB pool utilisation stays under target; retry queue has an explicit bound with dropped-and-logged (not silent) behaviour.
  • Rollback: flag-gated, graduated limits that can be relaxed or disabled.

P2-2. Give the database connection pool protection and better isolation.

  • Owner: Database owner.
  • Verification signals: pool saturation no longer reaches 100% under retry load; critical paths (checkout/job intake) keep a reserved share; pool wait times stay under a defined SLO under the same load; remaining connection slots reserved does not recur.
  • Rollback: pool-size and reservation changes are config-only and reversible; capacity headroom should be sized from measured load, not from the 09:20 event alone (peak was 300 by definition, which is a ceiling not a measurement of need).

P2-3. Fix the operational response path.

  • Owner: Incident lead (runbook), Release engineering (tooling).
  • Verification signals: a runbook entry stating that rising queue depth plus falling worker CPU plus DB pool pressure means contention, not capacity — so do not scale out and do not rely on a deploy rollback to disable a flag; a triage decision tree includes "list recently changed flags/config" as an early step; a game-day run of this scenario shows the flag check happening within ~10 minutes of first alert.
  • Rollback: documentation and tooling only.

P2-4. Per-tenant policy dashboards and config provenance.

  • Owner: SRE / observability.
  • Verification signals: for each tenant you can see the resolved policy, its source, and last change time; mismatch between default and resolved policy is visible; a config write is attributable to a change/rollout.

P3 — Process and follow-through

P3-1. Blameless postmortem with a written timeline, including why the 09:14 scale-up and the 09:24 rollback were chosen and what information would have led to the flag check sooner.

  • Owner: Incident lead. Verification: review held, actions assigned with owners and dates inside 5 working days.

P3-2. Customer-facing remediation decision based on the A6 duplicate-execution reconciliation.

  • Owner: Support / customer ops with Service owner. Verification: affected tenants contacted if impact is confirmed; no unexplained silent impact.

P3-3. Review other feature-flag-gated config paths for the same "empty value silently becomes aggressive behaviour" pattern.

  • Owner: Service owner (API) and Service owner (worker runtime). Verification: a list of flag-gated config paths audited, each with a test for the missing-value case.

C. Sequencing

  1. A1–A6 immediately (read-only or flag-only; cheapest and fastest risk reduction).
  2. P1-1 and P1-2 next — the defect fix and the alert that would have paged ~09:07 instead of 09:43.
  3. P1-3 and P2-1/P2-2 as the durability layer; P2-3/P2-4 to shorten future diagnosis.
  4. P3 after the postmortem, once impact scope is known.

D. Cross-cutting caution

The provided evidence shows the tenant retry policy feature to be the trigger, but it does not identify the precise code or configuration defect, nor does it quantify user impact. Actions that change capacity (P2-1, P2-2) or retry behaviour (P1-1, P2-1) should therefore be validated with a targeted load test reproducing zero-delay retry volume before they are relied upon, rather than assuming the fix addresses every contributing factor.

step-5-preview/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Status update: processing delays in the EU region

From approximately 09:07 UTC to 10:18 UTC, background jobs in our EU region took longer than usual to complete. Some customers may have noticed slower job completion and slower checkout. New job submissions returned to normal at around 09:24 UTC, and the backlog was cleared by about 10:18 UTC. Our US region was not affected in the same way.

What happened: a recent release included a new setting controlling how quickly a job is retried when it does not succeed. In the affected region it was applied in a state where jobs retried far faster than intended. Those repeated retries consumed shared database connections, slowing processing overall.

What we did: we turned the setting off, and processing recovered within about two minutes.

Next: we are reviewing why the setting was applied incorrectly, checking whether any job ran more than once, and adding alerts on retry behaviour to catch this earlier.

No action is needed from you. We apologise for the disruption.

step-5-preview/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident report — queue backlog and checkout latency (eu-west)

Status: Mitigated (backlog cleared 10:18 UTC) Incident window: 09:07–10:18 UTC (≈1 h 11 m); alert window 09:11–10:18 UTC (≈1 h 7 m) Primary region: eu-west. US region raised no alerts. Sources: TIMELINE.md, LOGS.txt. Nothing outside these two files is treated as fact.

1. Impact

Observed (from timeline/logs):

  • Rising queue depth in eu-west from 09:07; worker CPU fell from 65% to 18%.
  • Alerts fired at 09:11 for job age and checkout latency.
  • Database connections hit the configured maximum of 300 at 09:20.
  • An API worker reported db remaining connection slots reserved at 09:19:52.
  • Backlog was not cleared until 10:18, i.e. 33 minutes after the effective mitigation at 09:43.
  • New job creation normalized only after the API deploy was rolled back at 09:24.
  • US region: same release, flag enabled for ~2% of tenants, no alert fired.

Inference (not directly measured in the provided data):

  • Because latency and job age are user-visible signals, customer-facing impact most likely includes delayed checkout/job completion during the alert window; the exact number of affected jobs, tenants, and orders is not in the provided data.
  • Zero-delay retries of jobs that fail (or time out) create a risk of duplicate side effects for non-idempotent jobs. No evidence in the provided data confirms or excludes this.

2. Timeline (concise, UTC)

Time Event
09:02 Deploy api 2026.09.23.1. Change adds a tenant-specific retry policy.
09:06:58 worker-17: job 81 (tenant acme) retries 3× with delay_ms=0, ends retry_budget_exhausted policy="".
09:07:01 worker-22: db pool wait 5000 ms exceeded, active=10 idle=0.
09:07 Queue depth begins rising in eu-west; worker CPU 65% → 18%.
09:11 Alerts fire for job age and checkout latency.
09:14 On-call scales workers 40 → 90; queue depth rises faster.
09:20 DB connections reach configured maximum (300).
09:24 API deploy rolled back; new job creation normalizes, backlog still stuck.
09:31 One worker pool restarted; config_retry_default="exponential" feature_flag=true.
09:31–09:35 Pool processes normally for ~4 minutes, then stalls again (09:35:12 retry_budget_exhausted policy="").
09:43 Feature flag tenant_retry_policy disabled. Recovery begins within 2 minutes.
09:43:09–10 Retries now show delay_ms=1000; job 319 succeeds (elapsed_ms=190).
10:18 Backlog cleared.

3. Most likely root cause

A defect in the flag-gated tenant-specific retry policy path caused retries to be issued with zero backoff instead of falling back to the default exponential policy. Tight retry loops multiplied per-job demand until the shared database connection pool (max 300) was saturated; job processing then starved (workers blocked on pool acquisition rather than consuming CPU), producing the rising queue depth and the checkout-latency alerts.

Why the logs point here:

  • Before recovery, retries carried delay_ms=0 and the resolved policy was empty (policy="").
  • Five minutes after the effective mitigation (09:43:09–10), the same job type shows delay_ms=1000 and a fast success. That is a natural experiment: the code was present before, during and after; the flag state is the variable that changed immediately before recovery.
  • worker-51 at 09:31:10 started with a healthy default (config_retry_default="exponential") yet still stamped policy="" at 09:35:12 while feature_flag=true. The empty value came from the tenant-policy resolution path, not from the default config.
  • The 41 ms total elapsed for three retries of job 81 shows the retries failed fast; amplification came from retry volume, not from long-held connections.

4. Hypotheses and evidence

# Hypothesis Supporting evidence Weakening / absent evidence Assessment
H1 Tenant retry policy resolved to an empty policy → zero-delay retry storm → DB pool exhaustion → backlog delay_ms=0 with policy="" twice, before and after a restart; retries switch to delay_ms=1000 within 2 min of the flag being disabled; same release in US with 2% tenant exposure produced no alert Root-cause log line is policy="" (empty), not an error — the data does not show why the policy resolved empty (missing config vs. lookup failure vs. merge bug) Most likely. Strong causal mechanism, but the specific defect site is not proven
H2 The deploy 2026.09.23.1 itself was broken Deploy at 09:02 precedes symptoms; first bad retries at 09:06:58 Behaviour tracked the flag, not the binary: the API was rolled back at 09:24 and the backlog stayed stuck, while disabling the flag at 09:43 recovered within 2 min Contributing trigger, not the root cause. Same code ran faultless with the flag off
H3 Database connection pool too small / leaking Pool saturation at 09:20 and active=10 idle=0, 5000 ms waits; remaining connection slots reserved for the API Recovery followed a config change, not any capacity or pool change; no evidence of leaked connections or long hold times in the provided logs Likely amplifier / secondary weakness. Cannot be excluded, but not supported as the primary cause
H4 Traffic / organic workload surge Retries are numerous No traffic volume metric anywhere in the provided data; US saw no alert on the same release; retry pattern is a config signature, not a load signature Weak. Needs traffic data to exclude (see RESPONSE.md)
H5 Worker hosts saturated / infra fault (CPU 65% → 18%) CPU fell as the queue rose Low CPU with high queue depth is the opposite of saturation — it indicates workers blocked on I/O (waiting on the DB pool), which is consistent with H1. No host-level or noisy-neighbour evidence is present Rejected as primary (it is a symptom of blocking)
H6 The 09:14 scale-up (40 → 90 workers) caused the saturation Queue depth rose faster immediately after Queue depth was already rising from 09:07 and pool waits exceeded at 09:07:01, ~7 minutes before the scale-up Rejected as cause; accepted as a contributing aggravator that deepened contention before the pool hit its cap

5. Contributing factors

  1. No safe fallback for a missing/empty policy. The default exponential config was healthy, but the flag path did not fall back to it — a missing value silently became "retry immediately". This is the highest-leverage fix.
  2. Retry storms amplified demand with no brake. Empty policy plus retries produced bursty DB demand that the pool had no headroom to absorb.
  3. Scale-up under pool contention. Adding workers while the pool was already tight increased contention and made the queue look worse; this likely brought the 09:20 saturation forward.
  4. Flag blast radius. The flag was on for effectively all eu-west tenants, versus ~2% in US. Gradual rollout, not an all-tenant switch, is what kept the US quiet.
  5. Rollback was not a mitigation. The API deploy rollback at 09:24 did not disable the flag, so ~19 minutes were spent on an action with no effect on the stuck backlog.
  6. Restart was not a mitigation. Restarting a worker pool at 09:31 read the flag as still true, so the failure returned after ~4 minutes. Nothing in the provided data explains that 4-minute delay (config cache TTL? load ramp?).
  7. Detection gap. Nothing alerted on the actual failure signal (delay_ms=0 / empty policy / retry_budget_exhausted); the first alerts were downstream symptoms at 09:11, and the effective fix came at 09:43 — 32 minutes later.

6. What remains unknown

  1. Why the tenant policy resolved to empty — missing config entry for eu-west tenants, a failed lookup, a merge/override bug, or a partially-written config rollout. No config-store or policy-resolution log is in the provided data.
  2. What produced the ~4-minute healthy window after the 09:31 restart before the same stall returned.
  3. Whether the DB pool also leaked (long-held connections or slow queries), independent of the retry volume.
  4. Whether jobs ran more than once during the zero-delay retry storm (duplicate side effects, e.g. duplicate charges, emails, or writes).
  5. Blast radius in user terms — affected tenant count, failed/delayed job count, and any checkout orders that were abandoned or failed. No traffic or business-impact metric is provided.
  6. Which tenants/policies existed in eu-west versus the ~2% US exposure, and whether those 2% showed sub-threshold degradation that no alert captured.
  7. Whether the 09:14 scale-up caused avoidable additional harm beyond deepening contention (e.g. earlier saturation, connection churn, or queue-ordering effects).

Incident actions

Immediate containment

  1. Keep tenant_retry_policy disabled across regions until policy resolution and backoff are corrected. Owner: Incident Commander / on-call service owner. Verify: queue depth and oldest-job age fall; job throughput recovers; DB pool waits and active connections return below their limits; checkout latency returns to baseline. Rollback: If disabling the flag causes a separate, verified customer-impacting regression, use the last known-safe retry behavior and re-evaluate with the Incident Commander; do not restore the affected policy path without a canary.
  2. Hold worker concurrency at a controlled level and avoid further blind scale-ups while database connections are saturated. Owner: SRE on-call. Verify: connection usage stays below the configured cap, pool wait time falls, and completed-job throughput exceeds incoming work. Rollback: Restore the previous worker count if the cap worsens backlog drain after DB headroom is confirmed; scale in measured increments with connection monitoring.

Priority Action Owner role Verification signal Rollback consideration
