Service owner / SRE lead Tabletop or game-day confirms responders can identify the retry pattern, contain it, and verify backlog drain without exceeding DB capacity. Revert confusing runbook edits and retain the last reviewed procedure; no runtime rollback required.

Do not declare full recovery until queue depth and oldest-job age are normal, DB pool waits are clear, and checkout latency is stable. Confirm job completion and integrity before closing the incident.

gpt-6-luna/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Customer update

On September 23, some customers in our EU region experienced slower checkout responses and delays in queued work. We traced the issue to a recently enabled retry-policy path that caused some jobs to retry too quickly and contributed to pressure on our processing and database capacity. We disabled that path at 09:43 UTC, processing recovered, and the backlog was cleared by 10:18 UTC. We are reviewing the retry behavior and adding safeguards before enabling it again. We have not yet confirmed the full number of affected customers or whether any jobs were lost or duplicated. We will share an update if our review finds additional customer impact.

gpt-6-luna/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Queue incident analysis

Impact

Observed: The timeline records a growing eu-west queue from 09:07 UTC, alerts for job age and checkout latency at 09:11, and database connections reaching the configured maximum of 300 at 09:20. Rolling back the API deployment normalized new job creation, but the backlog remained stuck. Recovery began within two minutes of disabling tenant_retry_policy at 09:43; the backlog was cleared by 10:18. The US region ran the same release with the flag enabled for 2% of tenants and had no alert.

Inferred: EU customers likely experienced slower checkout and delayed queued work. The evidence does not establish the number of affected customers or jobs, whether any jobs were lost, or whether checkout requests failed. The incident window for the reported backlog was about 71 minutes (09:07–10:18 UTC); checkout impact may have started later or ended earlier.

Concise timeline (UTC)

  • 09:02 — Observed: API 2026.09.23.1 deployed with a tenant-specific retry policy change.
  • 09:06:58–09:07 — Observed: A worker logged three immediate retries for one job (delay_ms=0), then retry_budget_exhausted with an empty policy. Queue depth began rising and worker CPU fell from 65% to 18%.
  • 09:11 — Observed: Job-age and checkout-latency alerts fired.
  • 09:14 — Observed: Worker count increased from 40 to 90; the queue then rose faster.
  • 09:20 — Observed: Database connections reached the configured maximum of 300; a worker log also shows a 5-second DB-pool wait.
  • 09:24 — Observed: API rollback normalized new job creation, but the backlog remained stuck.
  • 09:31–09:35 — Observed: A restarted worker pool processed normally for four minutes, then stalled. Startup logged the default retry mode as exponential while the feature flag was true; a later job again exhausted its retry budget with an empty policy.
  • 09:43 — Observed: The flag was disabled. Within two minutes recovery began. The same job then retried after 1,000 ms and succeeded on its second attempt.
  • 10:18 — Observed: Backlog cleared.

Most likely root cause

Inference — moderate-high confidence: The feature-flagged tenant retry-policy path most likely caused or enabled the eu-west failure. The strongest mechanism indicated by the logs is that an empty/unresolved tenant policy allowed rapid retries with zero delay, exhausting retry budgets within tens of milliseconds and adding pressure while workers were already waiting on the database. Disabling the flag changed the observed retry behavior to a 1,000 ms delay and was followed closely by recovery. The supplied evidence does not prove the precise code defect or establish that retries alone caused the database connection limit to be reached.

Hypotheses and evidence

H1: The flagged tenant retry-policy path triggered retry amplification and queue stalls — most likely

  • Supports (observed): The release introduced the policy change; a worker logged three zero-delay attempts followed by an exhausted budget and policy="". After a restart, the flag remained true and the same empty-policy warning recurred. Following flag disablement, the logged retry used a 1,000 ms delay and succeeded. The timeline says recovery began within two minutes of disablement.
  • Weakens / limits: Only a few log lines are provided, with no retry-rate totals, tenant-level policy resolution, or direct trace from retries to DB connections. The logs do not show why the policy was empty. The 1,000 ms retry and success are one job, not proof that all jobs followed this path.

H2: Database connection exhaustion was the primary cause

  • Supports (observed): The connection limit of 300 was reached, and a worker reported no idle DB connections and a 5-second pool wait.
  • Weakens / limits: Queue growth and worker CPU decline began before the reported connection maximum. API rollback did not clear the stuck backlog, whereas flag disablement was followed by recovery. Database saturation is strongly supported as a proximate condition or contributing factor, but a separate database fault is not established.

H3: The API release caused a job-creation defect independent of the flag

  • Supports (observed): Queue growth began five minutes after the API deployment; rollback normalized new job creation.
  • Weakens / limits: The backlog stayed stuck after rollback and recovery followed flag disablement. The US region ran the same release without an alert at 2% flag exposure. This points toward exposure or workload differences, but does not isolate the flag because regional conditions are not provided.

H4: Insufficient worker capacity or a worker-pool defect was the initiating cause

  • Supports (observed): Worker CPU fell, scaling the pool coincided with faster queue growth, and a restart only helped temporarily.
  • Weakens / limits: These changes may be responses to the retry/DB pressure rather than independent causes. No worker saturation, scheduling, or pool-health diagnostics are provided. Increasing workers may have added concurrent DB demand, but that is an inference.

What remains unknown

  • The exact deployed code/configuration and how empty tenant policies were resolved.
  • Retry volume, retry delay distribution, affected tenants/jobs, and per-region flag exposure over time.
  • Whether database connection growth was caused by retry amplification, worker scaling, a leak, or another workload.
  • The number of customers affected, checkout failure rate, job loss/duplication, and any data integrity impact.
  • Why the worker pool stalled again after its restart and how much of the 10:18 recovery was due to backlog draining versus restored processing.

RESPONSE.md — analyst confidence and next data requests

Confidence level

Moderate (about 70%) that the root cause is the tenant_retry_policy path resolving an empty policy and issuing zero-delay retries that exhaust the retry budget and stall workers.

  • High confidence on the failure mechanism shown in logs (delay_ms=0, policy="", retry_budget_exhausted in ~40 ms) and on containment (flag off → recovery in ~2 min).
  • Moderate confidence on causation that this path started the outage (deploy/flag timing, US control group with 2% exposure and no alerts).
  • Low confidence on why policy resolution returned empty — that is the main gap between a plausible story and a proven root cause.

Facts vs inference: timeline entries, log lines, CPU/connection numbers, and flag state are observed. "Empty policy caused the retry loop" and "DB saturation was downstream" are inferences from ordering and correlation, not proof.

Next two pieces of data I would request

  1. Retry-policy resolution traces/config for tenant=acme (and 2–3 healthy tenants) around 09:00–09:45, including the raw policy store entries and code path that sets policy="". This would confirm or refute H1 directly.
  2. US region logs/metrics for the same window with the flag at 2% exposure, especially any delay_ms=0 or policy="" occurrences and DB pool waits. If US shows the same log pattern without impact, exposure-only correlation weakens; if US is clean, H1 and the exposure hypothesis both strengthen.

mimo/09-incident-analysis/ACTIONS.md

Actions — queue incident 2026-09-23

Immediate containment (done or do now)

  1. Keep tenant_retry_policy disabled globally until the empty-policy path is fixed. Owner: On-call SRE. Verify: no retry_budget_exhausted policy="" log lines; queue age falling. Rollback: re-enable flag only behind a 1% canary.
  2. Do not scale workers further while retry thrash is unresolved. Owner: On-call SRE. Verify: DB active connections < 250. Rollback: N/A (safety hold).
  3. Drain/inspect jobs that exhausted retry budget in 09:00–09:45 (e.g. job=81). Owner: Checkout Platform on-call. Verify: no silent drops; DLQ or retry status accounted for. Rollback: restore jobs from DLQ if dropped.

Prioritized corrective actions

P0 — Fix empty-policy retry fallback

  • Add a hard default: when tenant policy resolves empty, use the global exponential backoff (config_retry_default), never delay_ms=0. Cap attempts and enforce minimum backoff > 0. Owner: Checkout Platform (API/retry library). Verify: unit/integration test — empty policy ⇒ delay_ms>=1000 exponential; staging log shows policy never empty in the retry path. Rollback: revert library version behind a config flag; default stays safe.

P0 — Fail loudly on policy resolution failure

  • Emit ERROR + metric (retry_policy_resolution_failure) on empty resolution; alert on rate > 0. Owner: Checkout Platform + Observability. Verify: synthetic misconfigured tenant produces ERROR and alert within 5 min. Rollback: revert alert rule (keep log).

P1 — Guard retry load before it reaches the DB

  • Per-tenant and per-worker retry rate limit / circuit breaker; shed or defer retries under DB pool pressure (pause retries when pool wait > threshold). Owner: Checkout Platform. Verify: load test — retry storm holds DB connections < 200 and queue age stable. Rollback: disable limiter via flag retry_guard.

P1 — DB connection budget and scale-out safety

  • Reserve connection slots for non-retry paths (e.g. checkout writes); alert at 80% of 300; require DB headroom check before worker scale-out runbooks. Owner: DBA / SRE. Verify: alert fires in staging at 240 conns; scale-out runbook includes check. Rollback: restore previous pool limits.

P1 — Canary and flag exposure limits

  • Require canary (≤2% tenants) for flag-gated policy changes before regional rollout; EU/US parity checks. Owner: Release Engineering. Verify: release checklist blocks full rollout without canary metrics; US-style low exposure can be reproduced in EU. Rollback: flag dial-down is the rollback path (already used).

P2 — Rollback runbook: flags ride with deploys

  • Bundled rollback must disable related feature flags and restart worker pools that cached flag/config (see 09:31 stall). Owner: SRE / Platform. Verify: game-day — rollback clears backlog signals within 10 min. Rollback: restore previous runbook.

P2 — Retry observability

  • Per-attempt metrics (delay, policy name, budget remaining) and dashboard panels for delay_ms=0 rate. Owner: Observability. Verify: dashboard shows the 09:06 pattern within 30 s of onset in replay test. Rollback: keep logs; remove dashboard.

Verification signals (summary)

  • No retry_budget_exhausted policy="" lines.
  • Worker CPU in normal band while queue drains (not collapsed to <20%).
  • DB connections stable below 250 under peak.
  • Queue age and checkout latency SLOs recovered and hold for 30 min after any flag change.
  • retry_policy_resolution_failure metric rate is zero.

Rollback considerations

  • Every fix above ships behind a flag or is version-revertible; P0 fallback default must be safe when the flag is off.
  • Never roll back code without also flipping the associated feature flags and restarting worker pools (config caching caused the 09:31 false recovery).
  • Roll back in this order: disable flag → revert code → scale workers back to baseline only after DB headroom is confirmed.

mimo/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Customer update — September 23 checkout delays

Between roughly 09:07 and 10:18 UTC on September 23, some checkout and order-processing jobs in our EU region were delayed. A system that controls how failed jobs are retried appears to have been retrying them too quickly after a change we released that morning, which backed up our job queue and database connections. We rolled back the release and turned off the related setting around 09:43 UTC; processing recovered within minutes and the backlog cleared by 10:18 UTC.

We are still confirming details, including exactly which accounts saw delays and whether any orders need to be reprocessed. Our US customers were largely unaffected.

We are adding safeguards so retries slow down automatically if this kind of fault happens again, and we will notify you directly if we find that any of your orders need action on your side. We are sorry for the disruption.

mimo/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident analysis — queue backlog, eu-west, 2026-09-23

Impact (observed)

  • Queue depth rose in eu-west starting 09:07 UTC; job-age and checkout-latency alerts fired at 09:11.
  • Checkout latency degraded for customers whose jobs entered the queue during the window.
  • Database reached its configured maximum of 300 connections at 09:20 (API error: db remaining connection slots reserved).
  • Backlog did not clear until 10:18 UTC. Approximate customer-facing duration: ~71 minutes of degraded checkout processing (09:07–10:18), worst between 09:11 and 09:45.
  • US region: same release, tenant_retry_policy enabled for only 2% of tenants; no alert fired (no observed impact there).

Concise timeline (observed, UTC)

Time Event
09:02 Deploy api 2026.09.23.1 (adds tenant-specific retry policy)
09:06:58 Logs: job=81 (tenant=acme) retried 3× with delay_ms=0; retry_budget_exhausted policy="" in 41 ms
09:07 Queue depth rises in eu-west; worker CPU falls 65% → 18%
09:07:01 Logs: worker-22 DB pool wait >5 s (active=10 idle=0)
09:11 Job-age and checkout-latency alerts fire
09:14 Workers scaled 40 → 90; queue depth then rises faster
09:19:52 Logs: api-03 DB connection slots exhausted
09:20 DB connections at max (300)
09:24 API deploy rolled back; new job creation normalizes; backlog remains stuck
09:31 One worker pool restarted; processes normally ~4 min, then stalls
09:35:12 Logs: worker-51 (started with feature_flag=true) hits retry_budget_exhausted policy=""
09:43 Feature flag tenant_retry_policy disabled; recovery begins within ~2 min
09:43:09–10 Logs: job=319 retries with delay_ms=1000, then succeeds
10:18 Backlog cleared

Most likely root cause (inference)

The tenant-specific retry policy path behind tenant_retry_policy fails to resolve a policy for some tenants and falls back to zero-delay retries with an empty policy (policy="", delay_ms=0). This burns the retry budget in tens of milliseconds and re-enters jobs in a tight loop, stalling workers (CPU drops) while flooding the DB pool — which then exhausts the 300-connection ceiling and blocks checkout processing.

This is an inference from the correlation of deploy/flag state with symptoms plus the direct log evidence of the zero-delay retry pattern; it is not proven by a root-cause code review.

Hypotheses and evidence

H1: Tenant retry policy resolves empty → zero-delay retry loop (most likely)

Supporting (observed):

  • Deploy 09:02 introduced the policy; symptoms begin 09:07.
  • Logs show attempts with delay_ms=0 and retry_budget_exhausted policy="" within ~40 ms.
  • After the flag is off, the same job type retries with delay_ms=1000 (the configured default exponential) and succeeds.
  • Flag off at 09:43 → recovery within 2 minutes.
  • US: same binary, flag at 2% exposure → no alert (exposure tracks with impact).
  • Worker CPU fell while queue grew — consistent with workers blocked in rapid retry/DB-wait loops, not doing useful work.

Weakening:

  • We have not seen the policy-resolution code path; policy="" could be a logging defect rather than an empty policy.
  • EU exposure percentage is not stated (inferred from US being 2% and unaffected).
  • No direct proof that zero-delay retries (vs. another flag-gated behavior) caused the stall.

H2: Worker capacity shortage

Supporting:

  • Queue depth grew, and scale-up was attempted. Weakening (stronger):
  • Scaling 40→90 made queue depth rise faster and coincided with DB exhaustion — more workers amplified load rather than draining it.
  • Worker CPU dropped to 18%; workers were not CPU-bound.

H3: Database as root cause

Supporting:

  • DB hit max connections at 09:20; API errors show slot exhaustion. Weakening (stronger):
  • DB pool waits appear at 09:07:01, before the 09:20 ceiling and concurrent with the first retry-loop logs — DB saturation is a downstream consequence of retry thrash plus scale-out, not the first symptom.

H4: API deploy binary alone is broken

Supporting:

  • Rollback at 09:24 normalized new job creation. Weakening:
  • After rollback the backlog stayed stuck, and a worker pool restarted at 09:31 with feature_flag=true stalled again at 09:35 — the flag-gated path kept failing independently of the API deploy.
  • US ran the same release with low flag exposure and stayed healthy.

Contributing factors (observed or strongly inferred)

  1. No backoff when policy is empty (delay_ms=0); retry budget exhausted in ~40 ms.
  2. Silent policy-resolution failure (policy="") with only a WARN.
  3. Scale-out at 09:14 increased concurrent DB demand and retry traffic during the fault.
  4. DB connection ceiling (300) turned worker/API stalls into a broader outage.
  5. Broad tenant_retry_policy exposure in eu-west (vs 2% in US) concentrated impact.
  6. Deploy rollback alone did not disable the flag, prolonging the stuck backlog.

What remains unknown

  • Why policy resolution produced policy="" for tenant=acme (missing override, bug, or fallback design).
  • Exact EU flag exposure percentage at 09:02–09:43.
  • Whether exhausted-budget jobs were dropped, dead-lettered, or re-queued (job=81 fate unknown).
  • Why the 09:31 worker pool worked ~4 minutes before stalling (trigger for the 09:35 failure).
  • Why rollback at 09:24 did not clear the backlog (stuck jobs, workers still on new code, or requeue loops).
  • Whether impact extended beyond checkout (other job types) or beyond eu-west at low level.
  • Whether policy="" is also present in healthy US traffic (would weaken H1).

General rollback guidance

  • The flag disable (C1) is the primary, proven rollback and stays in place until A1/A2 are verified; re-enablement is itself a risky "forward" change and must be staged.
  • Any code fix (A1) ships dark behind a new flag with a canary; if the fix misbehaves, disable its flag rather than redeploying.
  • Prefer hot-reloaded config changes over restarts; restarts lose in-flight job state and caused a ~4-minute false recovery during the incident.

glm-5.3/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Customer Update

On 23 September, between roughly 09:07 and 10:18 UTC, some customers in our European region may have experienced slower checkout processing.

Our current investigation indicates that a recently introduced configuration setting caused background jobs to retry immediately instead of waiting between attempts. That extra load made database connections unavailable at times, which delayed order processing. Orders were still being accepted throughout this period, and we are reviewing individual orders to confirm none were missed; we will follow up directly with any affected accounts if needed. Our understanding may evolve as the review continues.

At 09:43 UTC we turned the setting off, processing began returning to normal within two minutes, and the backlog was fully cleared by 10:18 UTC.

We are making changes so that invalid retry settings fall back to safe defaults instead of fast retry loops, and we are improving monitoring to catch this pattern earlier. We are sorry for the inconvenience and appreciate your patience.

glm-5.3/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident Analysis — eu-west queue/checkout degradation (2026-09-23)

Impact

  • Customer-facing: elevated checkout latency in the eu-west region starting ~09:07 UTC; job-age and checkout-latency alerts fired at 09:11 UTC. Recovery began ~09:45 UTC; backlog cleared 10:18 UTC (~70 minutes of degraded service).
  • Internal: job processing backlog in eu-west; database connections saturated (max 300); workers stalled at ~18% CPU.
  • Blast radius: eu-west only. US ran the same release with tenant_retry_policy enabled for 2% of tenants and had no alerts (observed).
  • Unknown: whether any jobs were lost vs. only delayed (see "What remains unknown").

Concise timeline (UTC)

Time Event (observed)
09:02 API release 2026.09.23.1 deployed; adds tenant-specific retry policy
09:06:58 worker-17: job=81 (tenant=acme) retries 3x with delay_ms=0, then retry_budget_exhausted policy="" after 41 ms
09:07 eu-west queue depth rises; worker CPU falls 65% → 18%
09:07:01 worker-22: db pool wait 5000 ms (active=10 idle=0)
09:07:03 worker-08: job=92 (tenant=beta) succeeds — not all tenants affected
09:11 Alerts fire: job age, checkout latency
09:14 Workers scaled 40 → 90; queue depth rises faster
09:19:52 api-03: db remaining connection slots reserved
09:20 DB connections reach configured max of 300
09:24 API rolled back; new job creation normalizes; backlog still stuck
09:31 One worker pool restarted; processes normally ~4 min, then stalls
09:35:12 worker-51: retry_budget_exhausted policy="" — same signature post-restart
09:43 Flag tenant_retry_policy disabled; recovery begins within 2 min
09:43:09–10 worker-51: job=319 now retries with delay_ms=1000, succeeds on attempt 2
10:18 Backlog cleared

Observed facts vs. inference

Observed (from TIMELINE.md / LOGS.txt): every timeline row above; policy="" on exhausted retries; delay_ms=0 retries before the flag disable and delay_ms=1000 after; feature_flag=true read at worker-51 startup (09:31:10) and config_changed feature_flag=false at 09:43:07.

Inference (labeled): everything under "Hypotheses" and "Root cause" below; e.g., "workers were blocked on DB connections" is inferred from low CPU + pool-wait logs, not directly measured.

Most likely root cause

The tenant_retry_policy feature (release 2026.09.23.1, flag enabled broadly in eu-west) resolved to an empty policy (policy="") for some tenants, producing zero-delay retry loops that exhausted retry budgets (~40 ms) without job progress and rapidly cycled database work, saturating the 300-connection database cap. Workers then blocked waiting for connections (CPU 65%→18%), so the queue grew while workers idled.

Hypotheses, with supporting / weakening evidence

H1 — Empty tenant retry policy → zero-delay retry storm (root cause). High confidence.

  • Supports: policy="" appears exactly on budget-exhausted events (09:06:58, 09:35:12); pre-disable retries use delay_ms=0, post-disable uses delay_ms=1000 with success; disabling the flag (09:43), not the API rollback (09:24), started recovery; worker-51 shows feature_flag=true at startup — the API rollback could not fix workers; US (2% exposure, same code) had no alerts (dose–response).
  • Weakens: recovery timing could coincidentally overlap backlog drain; job=92 (beta) succeeded at 09:07:03, so per-tenant policy data may be a confounder (H3); no direct code-level evidence of the fallback bug is in the logs.

H2 — Database connection saturation as primary cause. Medium confidence as amplifier only.

  • Supports: pool waits at 09:07:01; "remaining connection slots reserved" (09:19:52); connections hit max 300 (09:20); scaling 40→90 workers made queue depth rise faster (more contention for a fixed cap).
  • Weakens: a worker-pool restart restored normal processing for ~4 minutes while the DB was presumably still saturated, and the DB recovered only after the flag disable, not after DB-side action — so DB saturation appears to be a consequence, not the origin.

H3 — Tenant-specific bad config (e.g., acme) rather than the flag path itself. Medium-low confidence.

  • Supports: acme's job exhausted its budget; beta's job succeeded during the incident.
  • Weakens: disabling the single flag fixed processing for job=319 and region-wide recovery began within 2 minutes; if per-tenant data corruption were the cause, a flag kill shouldn't have resolved it. Cannot be fully excluded that acme's config triggered the same code path.

H4 — Defect in release 2026.09.23.1 independent of the flag. Low confidence.

  • Supports: deploy at 09:02 precedes onset at 09:07.
  • Weakens: same release ran clean in US at 2% flag exposure; the API rollback at 09:24 did not restore backlog processing; recovery required the flag disable.

Contributing factors

  • Zero-delay retries (delay_ms=0) instead of the configured default (config_retry_default="exponential") — no fail-safe when policy resolved empty.
  • Fixed DB max (300) with no per-service caps or backpressure; scaling workers (09:14) amplified contention.
  • Mitigation gap: rolling back the API did not propagate the flag change to workers (flag read at worker startup), delaying recovery by ~19 minutes.
  • Detection gap: first alerts (09:11) were downstream symptoms; no alert on the retry-storm signature (delay_ms=0, retry_budget_exhausted, CPU-drop-with-queue-rise).
  • Rollout asymmetry: staged 2% exposure in US vs. broad enablement in eu-west.

What remains unknown

  • Why the policy resolved to "" — missing tenant config, migration gap, or fallback bug in the new code path.
  • The exact mechanism connecting zero-delay retries to DB saturation (do retries hold connections?) and what the 4-minute healthy window after restart represents.
  • eu-west flag exposure percentage (broadly enabled is inferred from region-wide impact, not measured).
  • Whether retry_budget_exhausted jobs were requeued (delayed) or dropped (lost) — job-level integrity unverified.
  • Per-service DB connection breakdown; whether api-03's "slots reserved" came from workers or the API.
  • Whether any customer checkouts failed outright, versus only being delayed.

mistral-large-4/09-incident-analysis/RESPONSE.md

Response summary

Confidence level

High that the tenant_retry_policy feature flag (release 2026.09.23.1) caused the incident, via a zero-delay / empty-policy retry loop that saturated the database connection pool and stalled worker processing. The evidence is consistent across three independent signals: (1) the zero-delay retry loop with policy="" appears in logs immediately before onset and recurs on a freshly restarted worker; (2) disabling the flag restored the exponential default (delay_ms=1000) on the same worker and recovery began within two minutes; (3) the dose-response pattern — 100% flag exposure in eu-west (incident) vs 2% in the US (no alerts). The rollback of the API alone at 09:24 did not fix processing, which rules out the API deploy as the primary cause.

Confidence is not absolute: the causal code path has not been inspected, so the exact mechanism producing policy="" and delay_ms=0 (empty tenant config vs. code fallback bug) is inference, and the worker CPU drop to 18% (blocked-on-pool vs. spinning) is unexplained. Treat the root-cause statement as "most likely" until the code diff and worker metrics confirm it.

Next two pieces of data I would request

  1. The release diff for 2026.09.23.1 — specifically the retry-policy code path: how the tenant-specific policy is loaded, what happens when it is empty/missing/unparseable (does it fall back to the exponential default or produce delay_ms=0?), and the worker config showing config_retry_default vs. the flag-controlled path. This would confirm or refute the root cause and distinguish "bad config" from "code bug."
  2. Worker-level metrics for 09:00-10:30 UTC — CPU breakdown (user vs. iowait vs. steal), DB pool wait time, connection acquire latency, and retry counts with delay histograms per worker, plus the DB-side view (connection count over time, top queries by frequency during the window). This would explain the CPU drop to 18%, confirm pool saturation as the proximate stall mechanism, and quantify the retry storm's load on the database.

mistral-large-4/09-incident-analysis/ACTIONS.md

Action plan — eu-west queue stall

Immediate containment (done during incident, for the record)

  1. Disable the tenant_retry_policy feature flag in eu-west — done 09:43; recovery began within two minutes (TIMELINE.md:11). Keep it disabled everywhere until the fix is verified.
  2. Hold the worker scale-up — the 40 → 90 increase at 09:14 made queue depth rise faster; do not add workers against a saturated DB pool. If scaling is ever needed again, pair it with a DB connection increase.
  3. Confirm backlog drain — backlog cleared at 10:18 (TIMELINE.md:13); verify no jobs were dropped (see verification below).
  4. Freeze release 2026.09.23.1 from further rollout; US is at 2% flag exposure — keep it there or roll the flag back to 0% pending the fix.

Prioritized corrective actions

P0 — Fix the retry policy fallback (blocks re-enable)

  • Action: Make the tenant-specific retry policy fall back to the built-in exponential default when the tenant policy is empty, missing, or unparseable; enforce a minimum retry delay (e.g., >= 100 ms) and add jitter. Treat policy="" as invalid config, not as "retry immediately."
  • Owner: Payments/Queue Engineering (team owning the worker retry library).
  • Verification signals: Unit/integration tests covering empty, missing, and malformed tenant policy all produce delay_ms >= 100 with the exponential default; in staging, a failing job shows non-zero, increasing retry delays and the retry_budget_exhausted log carries a non-empty policy string.
  • Rollback consideration: The fix is additive to the fallback path; if it misbehaves, re-disable tenant_retry_policy (known-good state, verified at 09:43) and keep the exponential default. No data migration involved.

P0 — Add a guard against zero-delay retry loops

  • Action: Reject or clamp delay_ms=0 retries at the worker level (config validation + runtime assertion), and cap per-worker concurrent retries / add a circuit breaker that pauses retries when DB pool wait exceeds a threshold.
  • Owner: Queue Engineering, with SRE review.
  • Verification signals: Staging fault injection (DB pool saturated) shows retries pause instead of hammering the pool; alert fires on retry delay_ms=0 events.
  • Rollback consideration: If the circuit breaker false-positives and delays legitimate jobs, loosen the threshold or disable the breaker only — the minimum-delay clamp can stay.

P1 — Alerting and detection

  • Action: Add alerts on: retry rate per worker, zero-delay retries (delay_ms=0), retry policy string empty, and DB pool wait time / saturation ratio. Current detection lagged onset by ~4-5 minutes (onset 09:07, alerts 09:11).
  • Owner: SRE / Observability.
  • Verification signals: In a staging game-day replay of this incident, the new alerts fire within 1 minute of retry-loop onset, before job-age alerts.
  • Rollback consideration: Alert-only change; tune thresholds if noisy. No rollback risk to production traffic.

P1 — Make scaling safe under pool saturation

  • Action: Add runbook guidance and, if feasible, an autoscaling guard: do not scale workers when DB pool utilization is high; scale DB connections (or read replicas / pooler) alongside workers.
  • Owner: SRE.
  • Verification signals: Game-day: scaling event during pool saturation no longer increases queue depth growth rate.
  • Rollback consideration: Runbook text only; the autoscaling guard can be disabled independently.

P1 — Staged flag rollout

  • Action: Roll out tenant_retry_policy (after the P0 fix) with the same staged approach that limited US exposure: 1% → 10% → 50% → 100% with soak periods and automatic rollback on the new retry/pool alerts.
  • Owner: Release Engineering with Queue Engineering.
  • Verification signals: Each stage soaks without retry-rate or pool-saturation alerts; canary dashboards clean.
  • Rollback consideration: Kill switch is the feature flag itself — proven effective at 09:43.

P2 — Clarify retry-budget semantics

  • Action: Determine whether retry_budget_exhausted jobs are re-queued or dropped (job 319 reappeared as attempt=1 after the flag change). If re-queued, confirm no duplicate side effects; if dropped, add a dead-letter path and alerting.
  • Owner: Queue Engineering.
  • Verification signals: Documented behavior plus a test showing exhausted jobs land in a dead-letter queue with an alert; reconciliation query shows zero lost jobs from the incident window.
  • Rollback consideration: Documentation/behavior change in worker only; if the dead-letter change misroutes jobs, fall back to previous re-queue behavior via config.

P2 — Post-incident verification of data integrity

  • Action: Reconcile jobs created 09:02-10:18 in eu-west against expected outcomes; confirm none failed permanently and quantify customer-visible checkout latency impact for the customer update and any credits.
  • Owner: Data/Support Engineering with SRE.
  • Verification signals: Reconciliation report with zero unexplained missing jobs; latency percentiles for the window published.
  • Rollback consideration: Read-only analysis; no rollback needed.

Standing rule for the next occurrence

Disable tenant_retry_policy first (fastest known mitigation, verified in this incident), then drain, then diagnose.

mistral-large-4/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Customer update — queue delays in eu-west (resolved)

Between 09:07 and 09:43 UTC today, job processing in the eu-west region was significantly delayed, which increased checkout latency for some customers. All delayed jobs were processed by 10:18 UTC, and the region is operating normally.

A configuration change in this morning's release caused background jobs to be retried without a proper delay, which overloaded our database connections and stalled the processing queue. We disabled the change at 09:43 UTC, and processing recovered within minutes. Other regions were not affected.

We have identified the cause and are fixing the retry behavior before re-enabling it, and we are adding earlier alerts so we can catch and stop this kind of issue faster. We apologize for the disruption and are reviewing the full impact on affected orders.

mistral-large-4/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident report — eu-west queue stall (2026-09-23 release)

Impact (observed)

  • Region: eu-west. US region ran the same release but with the flag enabled for only 2% of tenants; no alerts fired there (TIMELINE.md:14-15).
  • Degradation window: queue depth began rising at 09:07; recovery began within two minutes of the flag being disabled at 09:43; backlog fully cleared at 10:18.
  • Roughly 36 minutes of active degradation (09:07–09:43) and ~2h11m until the backlog was fully drained.
  • Alerts fired at 09:11 for job age and checkout latency (TIMELINE.md:5), indicating customer-visible checkout impact in eu-west.
  • Database connection pool saturated at its configured maximum of 300 at 09:20 (TIMELINE.md:7; LOGS.txt:7).

Concise timeline (observed, UTC)

  • 09:02 — API release 2026.09.23.1 deployed; adds tenant-specific retry policy.
  • 09:06:58 — worker-17 retries job 81 (tenant acme) three times in the same second, all delay_ms=0; retry budget exhausted with policy="" (LOGS.txt:1-4).
  • 09:07 — eu-west queue depth rises; worker CPU falls 65% → 18%. worker-22 reports DB pool wait exceeded (5000 ms), active=10 idle=0 (LOGS.txt:5).
  • 09:11 — Alerts fire for job age and checkout latency.
  • 09:14 — On-call scales workers 40 → 90; queue depth rises faster.
  • 09:19:52 — api-03 ERROR: DB remaining connection slots reserved (LOGS.txt:7).
  • 09:20 — DB connections reach configured maximum of 300.
  • 09:24 — API deploy rolled back; new job creation normalizes; backlog remains stuck.
  • 09:31 — One worker pool restarted; worker-51 starts with config_retry_default="exponential" feature_flag=true (LOGS.txt:8). Pool processes jobs normally for four minutes, then stalls.
  • 09:35:12 — worker-51 logs retry_budget_exhausted policy="" after 38 ms (LOGS.txt:9).
  • 09:43 — Feature flag tenant_retry_policy disabled; worker-51 sees feature_flag=false at 09:43:07 (LOGS.txt:10).
  • 09:43:09-10 — job 319 retries with delay_ms=1000 (the exponential default) and succeeds in 190 ms (LOGS.txt:11-12). Recovery begins within two minutes.
  • 10:18 — Backlog cleared.

Most likely root cause (inference, high confidence)

The tenant-specific retry policy introduced in release 2026.09.23.1 and gated by the tenant_retry_policy flag caused workers to retry failing jobs with zero delay (delay_ms=0) and an empty policy (policy=""), overriding the safe built-in exponential default. The resulting hot retry loop saturated the database connection pool (300/300 at 09:20), so workers blocked waiting for connections (CPU fell to 18%, db pool wait exceeded), and job processing stalled queue-wide. Disabling the flag restored the exponential default (delay_ms=1000) and processing recovered within two minutes.

This is inference from correlation and dose-response, not from reading the code: the code path that produces policy="" and delay_ms=0 has not been inspected.

Hypotheses and evidence

H1 — Tenant retry policy hot loop (empty/zero-delay policy) — SUPPORTED, most likely

Supporting:

  • LOGS.txt:1-4: three retries of job 81 within one second, all delay_ms=0, policy="" — a zero-delay retry loop, immediately before queue rise at 09:07.
  • LOGS.txt:8-9: a freshly restarted worker starts with config_retry_default="exponential" yet still logs policy="" at 09:35 — the tenant policy overrides the exponential default.
  • LOGS.txt:10-12: the moment the flag is disabled, the same worker retries with delay_ms=1000 and succeeds — direct before/after contrast on the same worker and job.
  • TIMELINE.md:11-13: flag disable at 09:43 → recovery within two minutes → backlog cleared.
  • TIMELINE.md:14-15: US ran the same release with the flag on for only 2% of tenants — no alerts. Dose-response pattern consistent with the flag being the cause.
  • TIMELINE.md:4: onset (09:07) follows the 09:02 deploy that introduced the policy.

Weakening / gaps:

  • Only a handful of log lines; we have not seen the config that yields policy="" (empty tenant policy vs. code default bug).
  • Correlation with the deploy is strong but the causal code path is unverified.

H2 — Database connection pool exhaustion is the root cause — WEAKENED (symptom, not cause)

Supporting: pool hit max 300 at 09:20 (TIMELINE.md:7); workers reported pool waits (LOGS.txt:5) and the API errored on reserved slots (LOGS.txt:7). Weakening:

  • Pool exhaustion occurred at 09:20, ~13 minutes after the queue began rising (09:07) and after the zero-delay retry loop was already logged (09:06:58). The pool saturation is downstream of the retry storm.
  • Disabling the retry flag — not adding DB capacity — is what restored processing.
  • Worker CPU fell to 18%, consistent with workers blocked on the pool rather than the pool failing on its own.

H3 — The API deploy itself / API-side regression — WEAKENED

Supporting: deploy at 09:02 preceded onset. Weakening: TIMELINE.md:8 — after the API rollback at 09:24, new job creation normalized but the backlog stayed stuck. The stall is in worker processing, not the API. Recovery required the flag change, which the API rollback did not include.

H4 — A single bad job or tenant (acme) caused the stall — WEAKENED

Supporting: job 81 / tenant acme is the first logged instance of the retry loop. Weakening: the impact was queue-wide (job age alerts, checkout latency, DB pool saturation across workers 17, 22, 51 and api-03). A single job cannot exhaust a 300-connection pool. Recovery came from a global flag change, not from removing acme's job.

H5 — Worker pool / worker host fault — WEAKENED

Supporting: one pool was restarted at 09:31 during diagnosis. Weakening: the restarted pool worked for four minutes and then stalled again with the same policy="" signature (LOGS.txt:8-9), and the issue resolved via flag change, not worker replacement. Multiple independent workers show the same behavior.

H6 — Underlying database outage — WEAKENED

Supporting: none direct. Weakening: jobs succeeded before 09:07 (LOGS.txt:6, success in 830 ms) and immediately after the flag disable (LOGS.txt:12, success in 190 ms) with no DB remediation. Only pool saturation was observed, which the retry storm explains.

Contributing factors (inference)

  • The tenant-specific policy overrides the safe exponential default rather than falling back to it; an empty/missing tenant policy yields policy="" and delay_ms=0 (LOGS.txt:8-9).
  • No effective backoff, jitter, or per-worker cap on concurrent retries, so a zero-delay loop multiplies DB load.
  • Scaling workers 40 → 90 at 09:14 amplified the problem: more workers generating zero-delay retries against an already-saturating connection pool, and queue depth rose faster (TIMELINE.md:6).
  • The retry loop appears to re-queue jobs after retry_budget_exhausted (job 319 reappears at 09:43 as attempt=1), sustaining the storm.
  • Detection gap: ~4-5 minutes between onset (09:07) and alerts (09:11); no alert on retry rate or delay_ms=0 directly.
  • Flag rollout was 100% of tenants in eu-west vs 2% in the US, which set the blast radius (TIMELINE.md:14-15).

What remains unknown

  • Why the policy string is empty: misconfigured/missing tenant policy entries vs. a code bug in the default path. Requires the release diff and tenant policy config.
  • The exact code path that maps tenant policy → retry delay, and whether delay_ms=0 is a fallback for a missing/parse-failed policy.
  • Why worker CPU fell to 18%: blocked on DB pool waits vs. spinning in a tight loop. Needs worker-level profiling/metrics (CPU breakdown, DB wait time).
  • Whether retry_budget_exhausted jobs are re-queued or dropped — affects whether any jobs were lost vs. only delayed.
  • Full blast radius: number of tenants/jobs affected, P99 job age during the window, and whether any jobs permanently failed.
  • Whether the US 2% cohort experienced elevated (sub-alert-threshold) latency or retry anomalies.
  • Why the restarted pool at 09:31 processed normally for four minutes before stalling (e.g., it took that long for the retry loop to re-saturate the shared pool).

gemini-3.1-pro/09-incident-analysis/RESPONSE.md

Incident Analysis Confidence and Data Requests

1. Confidence Level

Overall Confidence Level: High (90%)

Justification:

  • Failure Mechanism & Recovery (High Confidence — 95%):

    • The timeline and logs provide unambiguous causal evidence that the incident was triggered by zero-delay retries under the tenant_retry_policy feature flag.
    • In LOGS.txt, jobs under feature_flag=true (job=81 and job=319) logged delay_ms=0 and policy="", exhausting all retry attempts within ~40ms.
    • Immediate zero-delay retries caused database connection pool exhaustion (wait_ms=5000), blocking worker threads and driving worker CPU down from 65% to 18%.
    • Increasing worker instances from 40 to 90 directly saturated the database connection ceiling of 300, spilling contention over to the API tier (api-03 ERROR db remaining connection slots reserved).
    • Disabling the feature flag at 09:43 UTC immediately restored exponential backoff (delay_ms=1000), allowing jobs to complete and the queue backlog to clear within two minutes.
    • The US region, running the exact same binary release with only a 2% tenant flag rollout, experienced no alerts.

  • Underlying Software Implementation Defect (Moderate-to-High Confidence — 80%):

    • While the runtime behavior (policy="" and delay_ms=0) is conclusively established by the logs, the internal code mechanics responsible for generating an empty policy string (e.g., missing tenant mapping, database query returning null, or JSON deserialization omission) cannot be distinguished without inspecting the source code.

2. Next Two Pieces of Data Requested

  1. Source Code Diff for API Release 2026.09.23.1 (Tenant Retry Policy Module):

    • Specific Request: The pull request / commit diff that implemented the tenant-specific retry policy and feature flag evaluation.
    • Purpose: Determine why tenant=acme evaluated to policy="" and why the retry scheduler defaulted to delay_ms=0 rather than falling back to config_retry_default="exponential". This is necessary to implement and verify the code-level fix.

  2. Database Connection Pool Metrics & Error Telemetry (09:00–10:30 UTC):

    • Specific Request:
      1. Time-series connection pool metrics broken down by pool type / client service (API pool vs. worker pool connections, active vs. idle, wait queue duration).
      2. The detailed error message / stack trace for attempt 1 of job=81 and job=319.
    • Purpose: Identify what initial transient failure triggered the first retry attempt, and verify the precise connection allocation per worker instance to properly configure connection isolation quotas.

gemini-3.1-pro/09-incident-analysis/ACTIONS.md

Action Plan: Containment and Corrective Actions

This document outlines immediate containment actions and prioritized corrective measures to prevent recurrence of queue processing stalls and database connection exhaustion.

1. Immediate Containment Actions

1.1 Verify Regional Feature Flag State and Worker Scale

  • Description: Confirm that feature flag tenant_retry_policy is completely disabled (false) across all environments (including eu-west, US, and other regions). Ensure worker instance count in eu-west has been safely restored from 90 back to baseline nominal capacity (40 instances) to prevent database connection oversubscription.
  • Owner (Role): Incident Commander / Site Reliability Engineer (SRE) On-Call
  • Verification Signals:
    • Feature flag telemetry confirms feature_flag=false globally.
    • Worker pod count in eu-west returned to 40.
    • Total database connections stabilized comfortably below 150 (50% of the 300 maximum).
    • Worker CPU utilization sustained at normal operating baseline (55%–70%).
    • Queue depth is 0 and job age latency is at nominal baseline (<1s).
  • Rollback Considerations:
    • If queue processing starts falling behind at 40 workers under standard non-storm load, scale worker pool incrementally (e.g., to 50) while monitoring active DB connection counts. Do not scale beyond DB connection capacity.

2. Prioritized Corrective Actions

Priority 0 (P0) — Critical Defect Remediation & Capacity Protection

Action Item 1: Enforce Defensive Default Retry Backoff & Non-Zero Floor

  • Description: Patch the retry policy engine:
    1. If tenant-specific lookup returns null, empty string (""), or invalid configuration, explicitly fall back to config_retry_default ("exponential" with jitter).
    2. Implement an immutable lower-bound delay floor (min_delay_ms = 500) across all retry policies. Any policy configured with or evaluating to delay_ms < min_delay_ms must be rejected or clamped to prevent zero-delay retry loops.
    3. Validate tenant policy configurations with automated schema validation at deployment time.
  • Owner (Role): Backend Core / Queue Infrastructure Software Engineer
  • Verification Signals:
    • Unit tests verifying that missing, empty (""), or corrupt tenant policies default to exponential backoff.
    • Integration tests asserting that simulated transient job failures observe backoff with jitter and delay_ms >= 500.
    • Staging load tests verifying that failing tenant jobs do not saturate DB pools or trigger tight retry loops.
  • Rollback Considerations:
    • Deploy behind a new versioned feature flag. If regression occurs, toggle the flag off to keep the hardcoded system-wide exponential default active.

Action Item 2: Database Connection Pool Isolation (Bifurcation of API vs. Workers)

  • Description: Decouple database connection resources between customer-facing API checkout services and asynchronous queue workers:
    1. Implement connection pooling proxying (e.g., PgBouncer / ProxySQL) or partitioned database user pools.
    2. Allocate strict maximum connection quotas: e.g., max 120 connections reserved for API services, max 120 connections reserved for background workers, and 60 connections reserved for administrative and DBA maintenance.
    3. Enforce that worker autoscaling calculations cannot exceed the worker pool ceiling (max_instances * pool_per_worker <= worker_quota).
  • Owner (Role): Database Administrator (DBA) / Infrastructure Platform Engineer
  • Verification Signals:
    • Synthetic saturation tests in pre-production: Artificially saturating worker connection pools must leave API connection headroom unaffected, and checkout API transactions must complete without connection timeout errors.
    • Metric dashboards displaying separate connection utilization for API and Worker pools.
  • Rollback Considerations:
    • Pool limit adjustments can be reloaded in connection proxy configuration without database restarts. If quotas throttle legitimate peak traffic, pool allocations can be adjusted dynamically.

Priority 1 (P1) — Operational Guardrails & Deployment Safety

Action Item 3: Automated Autoscaling Guardrails & Runbook Update

  • Description:
    1. Update incident response runbooks for queue degradation: explicitly prohibit manual or automated scaling of worker instances when DB connection pool waits or database connection limits are saturated.
    2. Integrate pre-conditions into autoscaling policies: worker scaling should be capped or throttled if database connection utilization exceeds 75% or if worker CPU drops below 30% alongside high queue depth (indicating I/O blockage rather than compute shortage).
  • Owner (Role): Site Reliability Engineering (SRE) Lead
  • Verification Signals:
    • Documented and reviewed runbook in incident management repository.
    • Autoscaler policy simulation confirming that scaling is blocked when DB connection alerts are active.
    • Validation conducted during next operational game-day / chaos drill.
  • Rollback Considerations:
    • If autoscaler constraints prevent necessary scaling during sudden organic traffic surges, on-call retains manual emergency override capability with mandatory DB headroom verification.

Action Item 4: Regionally Consistent Canary Rollout Standards

  • Description:
    1. Enforce automated phased rollout policies for feature flags across all regions uniformly. Disallow releasing a feature flag broadly in one region (e.g., 100% in eu-west) while another region is at 2% canary.
    2. Implement Automated Canary Analysis (ACA) gates: feature flag promotion must automatically halt and roll back if regional metrics (queue age, worker CPU drops, DB connection wait times, error rates) breach standard anomaly thresholds within a 30-minute soak window.
  • Owner (Role): Release Engineering / DevOps Lead
  • Verification Signals:
    • Feature flag management platform configured with mandatory staged progression (e.g., internal -> 2% -> 10% -> 50% -> 100%) with automated health check evaluation.
    • Automated canary pipeline logs verifying automatic halt on synthetic error injection in staging.
  • Rollback Considerations:
    • Feature flag platform supports instantaneous 1-click global kill switch and automatic rollback on metric breach.

Priority 2 (P2) — Telemetry and Observability Enhancements

Action Item 5: Anomaly Alerts for Retry Velocity and Connection Starvation

  • Description:
    1. Add real-time Prometheus / Datadog alerts for rapid retry exhaustion: trigger high-priority alerts if any tenant or worker logs delay_ms == 0 or retry budget exhaustion (retry_budget_exhausted) within <100ms.
    2. Add proactive alerts for DB connection pool wait durations (wait_ms > 1000) and worker CPU-to-queue-depth divergences (high queue depth + low CPU).
  • Owner (Role): Observability / Monitoring Lead
  • Verification Signals:
    • Pre-production test triggering simulated zero-delay retries correctly trips the new alert within 60 seconds.
    • Alert routing validated to page the on-call queue infrastructure engineer.
  • Rollback Considerations:
    • Alert configurations managed via code repository; can adjust sensitivity thresholds or silence without service interruption.

gemini-3.1-pro/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Between 09:07 and 10:18 UTC, our Europe (eu-west) region experienced a service degradation affecting background job processing and checkout request speeds.

A newly deployed retry configuration caused certain background tasks to retry repeatedly without intended delays. This rapid retry activity placed high demand on database connections, creating a backlog in job processing and intermittently slowing checkout requests. Other regions, including the US, remained unaffected.

Our engineering team mitigated the issue by disabling the new retry configuration at 09:43 UTC, which allowed processing to recover. The job backlog was fully cleared by 10:18 UTC, and all services are operating normally.

We are implementing additional safeguards to enforce delay intervals during retries and isolating database connection capacity between background tasks and checkout services. We sincerely apologize for any inconvenience caused.

gemini-3.1-pro/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident Analysis Report: Queue Processing Stall and DB Connection Exhaustion

Executive Summary

On 2026-09-23 between 09:07 UTC and 10:18 UTC, the eu-west region experienced a major service degradation characterized by a queue processing stall, job backlog accumulation, and degraded customer checkout latency. The incident was triggered following deployment of API release 2026.09.23.1 with feature flag tenant_retry_policy enabled. A defect in the retry policy logic caused failing jobs for certain tenants to retry with zero delay (delay_ms=0), inducing a tight-loop retry storm that exhausted database connection pools and caused worker threads to block on I/O. An operational scale-up of workers from 40 to 90 exacerbated the database connection exhaustion to the global limit of 300, impacting the API tier. Full recovery occurred after disabling the feature flag tenant_retry_policy, restoring the default exponential backoff.

1. Impact

  • Geographic Scope: Isolated to eu-west. The US region, which had the same release but only 2% flag enablement, experienced no alerts or degradation.
  • Duration:
    • Degradation Start: 09:07 UTC (queue depth began rising, worker CPU dropped).
    • Mitigation Time: 09:43 UTC (feature flag disabled; recovery evident by 09:45 UTC).
    • Backlog Cleared: 10:18 UTC (total incident duration: ~71 minutes).
  • User & Business Impact:
    • Asynchronous job processing stalled in eu-west, triggering job age alerts.
    • Customer checkout latency spiked, triggering automated alerts at 09:11 UTC.
    • API checkout requests experienced database connection reservation failures (db remaining connection slots reserved) as DB connections hit the hard ceiling of 300 at 09:20 UTC.
  • System Telemetry Impact:
    • Worker CPU utilization dropped precipitously from 65% to 18% as threads became blocked waiting for database connections (wait_ms=5000).
    • Database connections reached the configured maximum of 300.

2. Concise Timeline (UTC)

  • 09:02: API release 2026.09.23.1 deployed, introducing tenant-specific retry policy logic.
  • 09:06:58: worker-17 processes job=81 for tenant=acme; attempts 1, 2, and 3 fail and retry immediately with delay_ms=0. Retry budget is exhausted in 41ms with policy="".
  • 09:07:00: Queue depth begins rising in eu-west; worker CPU falls from 65% to 18%.
  • 09:07:01: worker-22 logs WARN db pool wait exceeded wait_ms=5000 active=10 idle=0.
  • 09:07:03: worker-08 completes job=92 for tenant=beta successfully (elapsed_ms=830), showing non-retrying jobs can still run if connections exist.
  • 09:11: Automated alerts fire for job age and checkout latency.
  • 09:14: On-call engineer scales worker pool from 40 to 90 instances; queue depth rises faster.
  • 09:19:52: api-03 logs ERROR db remaining connection slots reserved.
  • 09:20: Database connections reach configured ceiling of 300.
  • 09:24: API release 2026.09.23.1 rolled back to prior version; new job creation normalizes, but worker backlog remains stuck.
  • 09:31: On-call restarts one worker pool; startup log (worker-51) shows config_retry_default="exponential" but feature_flag=true. Pool processes jobs normally for 4 minutes.
  • 09:35:12: worker-51 encounters job=319, exhausting retries in 38ms with policy=""; worker pool stalls again.
  • 09:43:07: Feature flag tenant_retry_policy disabled (feature_flag=false).
  • 09:43:09: worker-51 retries job=319 with fallback default policy, applying delay_ms=1000; attempt 2 succeeds in 190ms at 09:43:10.
  • 09:45: Backlog recovery begins across eu-west within two minutes of flag change.
  • 10:18: Queue backlog is completely cleared; metrics return to baseline.

3. Most Likely Root Cause

Separation of Observed Facts vs. Inference

  • Observed Facts:

    1. The API deploy introduced tenant-specific retry policies under feature flag tenant_retry_policy.
    2. In LOGS.txt, jobs failing under feature_flag=true (job=81 and job=319) logged policy="" and delay_ms=0, burning 3 attempts in ~40ms.
    3. Under feature_flag=false, job=319 logged delay_ms=1000 (exponential backoff) and succeeded on attempt 2.
    4. Worker CPU fell to 18% while DB pool wait timeouts (wait_ms=5000) occurred.
    5. Scaling workers from 40 to 90 pushed database connections to 300 and triggered API database connection reservation errors.
    6. Rolling back the API did not clear the stall, but turning off the feature flag immediately resolved it.
    7. The US region (2% rollout) experienced no alerts.

  • Inference (Root Cause Mechanism):

    • Defective Fallback / Missing Policy Evaluation: In API release 2026.09.23.1, when the tenant_retry_policy feature flag was active, the retry engine attempted to evaluate a tenant-specific retry policy. For tenants without a configured policy (or due to a lookup/parsing bug), the policy resolved to an empty string (policy="").
    • Zero-Delay Retry Storm: Instead of falling back to the configured default (config_retry_default="exponential"), the empty policy was interpreted as an unthrottled immediate retry (delay_ms=0).
    • Connection Starvation and Thread Blocking: When transient failures occurred, jobs executed retries instantaneously in tight loops. Each retry attempt held or requested database connections from the worker's pool. Because worker threads spent their time waiting for database pool slots (wait_ms=5000), worker CPU utilization plummeted (from 65% to 18%), and worker capacity to consume the queue dropped to zero.
    • Cascading Contention: Scaling workers to 90 multiplied active connection pools, maxing out the database (300 connections) and starving the API tier, which directly impacted checkout latency.

4. Contributing Factors

  1. Unsafe Default Value in Retry Engine: When a specific policy was missing or blank (policy=""), the system defaulted to delay_ms=0 rather than applying the global exponential backoff default or enforcing a non-zero minimum backoff floor.
  2. Counterproductive Autoscaling Intervention: Scaling workers from 40 to 90 without verifying DB connection headroom turned an asynchronous queue backup into a database connection saturation incident, spilling over into the checkout API.
  3. Shared Database Connection Ceiling: Both asynchronous background workers and latency-critical API checkout services competed for the same database connection allocation (300 max connections) without pool partitioning.
  4. Decoupled Deployment and Feature Flag Configuration: Rolling back the API release did not disable the feature flag in the runtime environment. Worker nodes retained feature_flag=true until the flag was explicitly toggled at 09:43 UTC.
  5. Canarying Discrepancy: While the US region was safely limited to a 2% tenant canary, eu-west had the feature flag enabled broadly (likely 100%), exposing the entire region to immediate failure upon deployment.

5. Evaluation of Hypotheses

Hypothesis Supporting Evidence Weakening Evidence Conclusion
1. Zero-delay retry policy bug causing DB pool exhaustion (Most Likely) • Direct log evidence: delay_ms=0 and policy="" in 09:06:58 and 09:35:12.
• Immediate recovery upon feature_flag=false with delay_ms=1000.
• US region with 2% flag showed zero alerts.		 • Some non-failing jobs (job=92 for tenant=beta) completed successfully at 09:07:03, confirming the issue required a job failure/retry to trigger the storm. Confirmed as Root Cause. Explains all observed symptoms, log entries, and the exact recovery timing.
2. Primary Database capacity exhaustion / hardware degradation • DB connections hit 300 at 09:20.
• Worker CPU drop (18%) matches DB I/O blocking.		 • DB saturation occurred after zero-delay retries started and after worker scale-up (09:14).
• API rollback did not resolve it, but changing retry logic did without modifying DB hardware.		 Rejected as Root Cause. DB saturation was a downstream symptom of the retry storm and worker scale-up.
3. Permanent "Poison Pill" job payload locking the queue • Backlog remained stuck after API rollback.
• Restarted worker pool stalled again after 4 minutes upon encountering job=319.		 • When feature_flag=false was applied, job=319 succeeded on attempt 2 in 190ms.
• The job was not intrinsically unprocessable; it only failed due to zero-delay retry exhaustion under transient contention.		 Rejected. Jobs were transiently failing and recoverable once backoff delays were honored.
4. Worker code crash / memory leak • Worker pool had to be restarted by on-call at 09:31. • Worker CPU was low (18%), not high.
• Logs showed active logging (INFO, WARN), not unhandled process crashes or OOM terminations.		 Rejected. Workers were blocked on connection pool waits, not crashed.

6. What Remains Unknown

  1. Root Cause of the Initial Transient Failure: Why did job=81 and job=319 fail on attempt 1? Was it an external API dependency timeout, transient database lock contention, or application error?
  2. Implementation Flaw in 2026.09.23.1: Did the empty policy (policy="") occur because tenant acme had no policy row in the database, because a configuration parser returned an empty string, or because the feature flag failed to bind tenant metadata?
  3. Feature Flag Architecture: How are feature flags propagated to worker pools? Why was the feature flag enabled broadly in eu-west while restricted to 2% in US?
  4. Worker DB Pool Allocation: What is the configured pool size per worker (e.g., active=10 in logs suggests 10 per worker; 40 workers = 400 potential connections, and 90 workers = 900 potential connections against a 300 DB limit)?

mcode-m3/09-incident-analysis/RESPONSE.md

Response — confidence and next data

Confidence

High that the tenant_retry_policy feature flag combined with an empty resolved policy (policy="") is the proximate trigger of the retry storm. The supporting evidence is three-fold:

  • Log signature: delay_ms=0 retries inside a single second, followed by retry_budget_exhausted with policy="", preceding the queue rise and DB pool saturation.
  • Differential blast radius: same release, same DB tier, but US (flag at ~2% of tenants) showed no alert, while eu-west (flag effectively at 100%) degraded.
  • Recovery: disabling the flag at 09:43 produced recovery within ~2 minutes on a freshly observed worker pool (worker-51), including a job which had previously failed and now succeeded on attempt 2 with a proper delay.

Medium on the underlying mechanism (e.g., why policy="" resolves to delay_ms=0 rather than a safe default) — the behaviour is consistent with the logs but the worker source has not been read in this analysis.

Medium on the customer-visible impact envelope. We know latency and job age regressed and the backlog eventually drained, but we have not yet quantified: dropped jobs, failed user actions, or the 2% of US tenants that may have had silent degradation.

Next two pieces of data I would request

  1. The worker source / config-resolution code path for the retry scheduler. Specifically the branch taken when policy="" or policy=null. This will either confirm the delay_ms=0 fallback hypothesis directly or surface a second mechanism that the logs hint at (the "4 minutes healthy then stalls" pattern on the restarted worker pool).
  2. The retry-budget and outcome data for the affected window. For every job processed by eu-west workers between 09:00 and 09:45 UTC, the attempt, delay_ms, terminal_outcome (success / exhausted / dropped), and tenant_id. This will tell us how many jobs exceeded their retry budget and were dropped, and whether the 2% of US tenants with the flag on saw any silent degradation — both of which are needed to size customer impact and to size the safety budget in Action 7.

11 Add structured retry telemetry: per-job delay_ms, policy, attempt, tenant, exposed as a metric and a dashboard. Observability Dashboard shows delay_ms distribution; alerting on delay_ms=0 rate > 0. Telemetry is additive; no rollback risk.

P2 — Within the quarter

# Action Owner (role) Verification signal Rollback consideration
12 Move the DB connection pool so API and worker paths have separate budgets, with a shared overflow gate. Database Team Load test: worker storm no longer starves API path. Pool split is reversible; traffic can be re-pooled behind a flag.
13 Add a chaos test that injects a delay_ms=0 retry storm on one tenant and asserts the system stays within SLOs. SRE Quarterly chaos drill passes; alert fires on injected storm. Test is read-only against prod traffic shape; rollback = disable drill.
14 Post-incident review document and circulate to engineering; track Action items to closure in the incident tracker. Incident Commander Review held within 5 business days; action items have owners and dates. N/A.

mcode-m3/09-incident-analysis/CUSTOMER_UPDATE.md

Service update — job processing delays in eu-west

Between approximately 09:00 and 09:45 UTC today, customers in our eu-west region may have experienced slower than usual checkouts and longer than usual delays for background jobs (such as order confirmations and notifications).

The issue was caused by a configuration change in a background-job processor that caused some jobs to be retried more aggressively than intended. This in turn saturated a shared database resource. Our team identified the change as the cause, disabled the affected setting at 09:43 UTC, and saw the system recover within minutes. The backlog of delayed jobs was fully cleared by 10:18 UTC.

Our other regions were not affected in any customer-visible way.

We are sorry for the disruption. No customer data was lost. We are following up with targeted fixes and additional safeguards before any similar change is re-enabled, and we will share a full post-incident summary internally.

mcode-m3/09-incident-analysis/INCIDENT.md

Incident Analysis — Queue API (eu-west)

Impact

  • Region affected: eu-west (primary). US was nominally exposed but the feature flag was enabled for only ~2% of tenants there, so no alert fired and no customer-visible impact has been confirmed.
  • Customer-visible symptoms: elevated checkout latency and rising job age in eu-west starting ~09:07 UTC. New job creation returned to normal after the API rollback at 09:24, but existing backlog continued to stall until ~10:18 UTC.
  • Blast radius: all eu-west tenants were processed by the new code path once the deploy reached steady state; however, the failure manifested as per-job retries rather than data loss. Whether any jobs exceeded their retry budget and were dropped permanently is not yet confirmed (see Unknowns).
  • Duration of customer impact: approximately 09:07 → 09:43 UTC (queue stalled, latency degraded), with full backlog drain at 10:18 UTC. End-to-end ~71 minutes of degraded service, ~35 minutes of severe backlog.

Concise timeline (UTC)

Time Event
09:02 Deploy of api 2026.09.23.1 adds tenant-specific retry policy (tenant_retry_policy flag).
09:07 Queue depth begins rising in eu-west; worker CPU falls 65% → 18% (workers stuck waiting).
09:07 Logs show delay_ms=0 retries on the same job inside a single second; retry_budget_exhausted logged with policy="".
09:07 DB pool wait exceeded 5,000 ms; 10 active connections, 0 idle.
09:11 Alerts fire: job age and checkout latency.
09:14 On-call scales workers 40 → 90; queue rises faster (negative feedback loop).
09:19 DB error: remaining connection slots reserved (pool saturated at 300).
09:24 API deploy rolled back; new-job creation normalizes, but existing backlog remains stuck.
09:31 One worker pool restarted; jobs process normally for ~4 minutes, then stall again.
09:35 retry_budget_exhausted logged again on the restarted pool (policy="").
09:43 Feature flag tenant_retry_policy disabled; recovery begins within ~2 minutes.
10:18 Backlog cleared.

Most likely root cause

A new tenant-specific retry policy shipped via the tenant_retry_policy feature flag was wired up such that when the flag was on and the resolved policy string was empty (policy=""), jobs retried immediately with delay_ms=0 rather than falling back to a safe default. Three retries of job 81 inside a single second (09:06:58) is the clearest smoking gun. This produced a retry storm in eu-west that saturated the shared database connection pool (300 max) and starved legitimate work, manifesting as a stuck queue with low worker CPU (the classic "blocked on downstream" pattern).

Two pieces of evidence strongly support this being the proximate cause:

  1. Temporal alignment: the delay_ms=0 retries and the first retry_budget_exhausted log (09:06:58) precede the queue-depth rise (09:07) and the DB pool saturation (09:07:01).
  2. Differential exposure: US ran the same release but had the flag enabled for ~2% of tenants. No alert fired there. If the deploy itself were the problem, both regions should have been affected equally.

Hypotheses ranked

H1 — Empty-policy retry storm (MOST LIKELY)

  • Claim: when tenant_retry_policy is on but the resolved policy is "", the worker falls back to a no-delay, multi-attempt loop.
  • Supporting evidence:
    • delay_ms=0 repeats in the same second for the same job (job=81, three lines, 09:06:58).
    • retry_budget_exhausted carries policy="" twice (09:06:58, 09:35:12).
    • Worker-51 starts up at 09:31 with feature_flag=true, config_retry_default="exponential", then stalls within ~4 minutes with the same policy="" exhaustion signature — and recovers within ~2 minutes of the flag being turned off (09:43).
    • Differential blast radius between regions matches the flag's rollout %.
  • Weakening evidence / gaps:
    • We have not yet read the worker source for the policy="" code path, so "fallback to no-delay retry" is an inference from log behaviour, not a confirmed code-level finding.
    • A worker pool restart at 09:31 looked healthy for ~4 minutes before stalling again, which is consistent with the flag being re-applied to new jobs that arrived after restart — but it is also consistent with a secondary resource (e.g., DB pool) being gradually exhausted. Both stories fit the data.

H2 — DB connection pool exhaustion as primary cause

  • Claim: something else caused DB pool saturation, and the retry storm is merely a downstream symptom.
  • Supporting evidence: pool saturation (remaining connection slots reserved) is real and preceded by 5,000 ms waits at 09:07:01.
  • Weakening evidence:
    • Pool saturation occurs after the delay_ms=0 retry burst, not before.
    • Scaling workers from 40 → 90 (09:14) made the queue worse, not better — consistent with each worker amplifying the storm, not with a static DB bottleneck being hit by extra connections.
    • US ran the same code and DB tier with the flag at 2% and did not alert.
  • Verdict: unlikely to be the primary cause; the pool saturation is best explained as a consequence of H1.

H3 — Deploy artifact / build issue (less likely)

  • Claim: the binary itself is broken independent of the flag.
  • Weakening evidence: US ran the same binary without impact proportional to eu-west. The flag toggle at 09:43 produced rapid recovery, which would not be expected if the binary itself were faulty.

Contributing factors

  1. No safe default for an unresolved policy. policy="" should have either inherited the existing default backoff or refused to start, instead of silently allowing delay_ms=0 retries.
  2. Flag rollout was not gated by tenant canary in eu-west. Going to 100% of eu-west tenants simultaneously removed the safety net that protected US.
  3. Worker auto-scale without a saturation circuit breaker. Scaling 40 → 90 workers amplified the retry storm and accelerated DB pool exhaustion instead of relieving it.
  4. Shared DB connection pool across API and worker paths. A retry storm in workers was able to starve API request capacity.
  5. API rollback at 09:24 did not affect workers. The retry config was read at worker startup and re-applied per job, so rolling back the API tier only stopped new jobs from being created — it did not change the workers' behaviour. This delayed real recovery by ~19 minutes.
  6. No automated rollback trigger. The feature flag was the correct lever, but it was disabled manually ~50 minutes after first impact.

What remains unknown

  • The exact code path that turns policy="" into delay_ms=0. Logs are consistent with a fallback bug but the worker source has not been read.
  • Whether the "4 minutes healthy, then stalls" pattern on the restarted pool is purely a function of new jobs arriving and re-triggering the storm, or whether there is a secondary slow-burn effect (e.g., a queue/cache).
  • Total number of jobs that exceeded their retry budget and were dropped vs retried successfully once the flag was disabled.
  • Customer-visible failure modes beyond latency (e.g., failed checkouts, abandoned carts, notification delays).
  • Whether US saw any impact at all on the ~2% of tenants with the flag on, even though no alert fired.
  • Why US and eu-west had such different flag rollout percentages (intentional canary or configuration drift).
